  ▘▘ ▝▝    ~/git/cm3/m3-sys/msir/test

❯ Let's design a Modula-3 Semantic IR that will allow us to retarget the CM3    
  compiler to LLVM. Design principles: Great — I’ll do all three, in a single   
  coherent narrative, so they reinforce each other rather than repeating        
  concepts.                                                                     
                                                                                
  1. Sketch of a Minimal Modula‑3 Semantic IR (MSIR)                            
  The goal of a Modula‑3 Semantic IR (let’s call it MSIR) is not to replace     
  LLVM, but to sit above LLVM while below the AST. Its purpose is to preserve   
  language meaning long enough to map it intentionally onto LLVM IR.            
  Think of it as “the last place where Modula‑3 still looks like Modula‑3.”     
                                                                                
  1.1 Design principles                                                         
  MSIR should be:                                                               
                                                                                
  Typed (stronger than LLVM, weaker than AST)                                   
  Structured (explicit constructs, not encoded control flow)                    
  Stable (not tied to LLVM version churn)                                       
  Lowerable (every node has a deterministic LLVM mapping)                       
                                                                                
  Avoid:                                                                        
                                                                                
  AST trivia (syntax)                                                           
  Backend artifacts (stack offsets, concrete layouts)                           

  Read 1 file, listed 4 directories (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Picking up where you left off — I'll continue the narrative through MSIR's
  structure, then cover lowering to LLVM, then the migration path from today's
  M3CG. Grounding everything in what's actually in the tree
  (m3-sys/m3middle/src/M3CG_Ops.i3 is 768 lines of stack-machine ops;
  m3-sys/m3back/src/M3C.m3 is the existing C backend; ex_stack already assumes
  Itanium-style EH).

  ---
  1.2 What MSIR keeps that M3CG threw away

  The current M3CG is a stack-machine API. By the time the front-end calls
  add(t), the operand types are already coerced, layouts already resolved to bit
   offsets, and there is no CFG — only labels and jumps. That is fine for
  emitting C, where the C compiler reconstructs everything. It is wrong for
  LLVM, where we want to hand the optimizer typed SSA values, structured EH, GC
  roots, and aliasing facts.

  MSIR should re-surface, as first-class IR, the things M3CG erases:

  - Typed values, not stack slots. Every MSIR value carries an MSIR type. No
  implicit widening; conversions are explicit nodes with a documented LLVM
  lowering.
  - A CFG of basic blocks with explicit terminators. No fall-through, no
  M3CG.label + M3CG.jump soup. Phi-nodes optional at this level (see §1.4).
  - Structured EH constructs. try_except, try_finally, raise, reraise as IR
  nodes, not Start_try/End_try/Landing_pad calls whose ordering is load-bearing
  (this is exactly the TryFinStmt.m3 footgun called out in CLAUDE.md).
  - Modula-3 type signatures preserved: open arrays carry their dope-vector
  shape; objects carry their dispatch vector; sets carry their domain bound;
  subranges carry min/max. M3CG flattens all of these to byte/bit math too
  early.
  - GC discipline as IR, not as a calling convention. Traced refs are a distinct
   type, write barriers are explicit ops, and roots are derivable from the IR
  without target-specific heuristics.
  - ABI as metadata, not as code. Things like "VAR parameter is by-reference"
  stay as parameter attributes; M3CG's pre-rewriting of these into
  address-of/load chains happens during lowering, not in the IR.

  1.3 Type system

  Three layers of types, each lowering deterministically to LLVM:

  MSIR type: Scalar
  Sketch: int<n>, word<n>, float<n>, bool, char, enum<UID,n>
  LLVM lowering: iN, iN, half/float/double/fp128, i1, i8/i16, iN
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  MSIR type: Aggregate
  Sketch: record{fields}, array[N] of T, set<lo..hi>, subrange<lo..hi> of T
  LLVM lowering: %struct, [N x T], iN (for small), [K x i64] (for large),
  backing
    scalar
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  MSIR type: Reference
  Sketch: ref traced T, ref untraced T, proc(sig), object{fields,methods,super},

    openarray T, text
  LLVM lowering: ptr addrspace(1) (GC), ptr, ptr to function-typed signature,
    {ptr,ptr} (fat pointer to obj+typecode) or just ptr with side metadata, {ptr
   
    T, [n x i64]} dope, ptr

  A few opinions worth committing to early:

  - Use an LLVM address space for traced pointers (e.g. addrspace(1)). It costs
  nothing on most targets, and it lets LLVM's GC infrastructure (gc.statepoint,
  gc.relocate) work later if you want a moving collector. CM3's current
  collector is non-moving on most targets, but baking traced-ness into the type
  prevents painting yourself into a corner.
  - EXTENDED is a parameter of MSIR, not a fixed type. Same way
  Target.Extended.size is parameterized today (and freshly, per the recent fix
  in m3middle/Target.m3). Lower to double everywhere except AMD64_LINUX, where
  it lowers to fp128 — exactly the rule in CLAUDE.md.
  - Subrange and enum stay distinct from their representation type. This buys
  you cheap range checks and TYPECASE/ISTYPE intrinsics that LLVM can fold but
  that iN alone wouldn't carry.
  - Open arrays are shaped, not shapeless. openarray T carries the rank, so
  multi-dim opens (ARRAY OF ARRAY OF T) lower without re-deriving the dope shape
   from the AST.

  1.4 Operations

  Roughly five families of ops; everything maps to one or more LLVM instructions
   or runtime calls:

  1. Value ops — iadd/isub/..., fcmp, convert, bitcast, extract/insert for
  records and arrays. Direct LLVM analogs.
  2. Memory ops — load, store, alloca, gep_field name, gep_index, plus gc.load,
  gc.store, gc.write_barrier. Lower to LLVM load/store/getelementptr with the GC
   variants going through statepoint intrinsics or a runtime call.
  3. Control ops — br, cond_br, switch, return. SSA phis or block parameters;
  either is fine — block parameters are easier to produce and trivially lowered
  to phis.
  4. M3 intrinsics — narrow, istype, typecase, dispatch method,
  set_union/set_intersect/set_member, range_check lo hi, nil_check,
  subscript_check, case_select. These are not runtime calls in MSIR; lowering
  decides whether to inline (small sets, constant ranges) or call into m3core.
  5. EH and concurrency — try_except {handlers}, try_finally, raise exc, arg,
  reraise, lock mutex { body }. Lower to LLVM invoke/landingpad/resume for
  ex_stack, or to setjmp/longjmp scaffolding for ex_frame. The IR doesn't pick —
   the lowering pass does, driven by the same Has_stack_walker table that exists
   today.

  The point of distinguishing (4) from runtime calls is that LLVM can see
  through them: a range_check on a constant range disappears; a dispatch on a
  known concrete object devirtualizes; a set_member on a small set folds to a
  single and+icmp.

  1.5 The Modula‑3-shaped sharp edges

  A few things that should be designed in from day one because retrofitting them
   is painful:

  - Module init order and FINALLY at top level. CM3's module initializer
  protocol (RTLinker__InitRuntime, then per-module init in dependency order)
  needs to be expressible. Treat module init as just a proc with a known
  signature; import_unit / export_unit become module-level metadata.
  - Nested procedures and up-level addressing. Either close over the activation
  explicitly (lambda-lifting at MSIR construction time) or carry an explicit
  static link. Lambda-lifting is simpler and friendlier to LLVM; CM3 is small
  enough that the cost is negligible.
  - WITH and VAR aliasing. Lower to address-taken locals with noalias/nocapture
  annotations where the front-end can prove non-aliasing — the front-end can
  prove it for WITH because the binding is lexical.
  - Packed records and bit-fields. Keep them as a structural type with explicit
  bitfield_load/bitfield_store ops; do not try to hand LLVM i7 types and pray.

  ---
  2. Lowering MSIR → LLVM IR

  Lowering is a single pass with a fixed shape:

  1. Type lowering is a pure function MSIRType → LLVMType plus a side-table for
  opaque/recursive types (objects, traced records).
  2. Per-procedure lowering walks the CFG once, producing LLVM blocks 1:1 with
  MSIR blocks. Block parameters become phi nodes at the join.
  3. EH lowering is target-conditional:
    - Has_stack_walker target → emit invoke/landingpad/resume. _M3Exc becomes
  the LLVM exception type; the existing C++ personality stays. The End_try
  placement bug class disappears because the IR carries the structure.
    - Other targets → emit a setjmp/longjmp scaffold equivalent to today's
  ex_frame. Same MSIR, different lowering pass.
  4. GC lowering at this stage is conservative: traced pointers stay in
  addrspace(1), gc.write_barrier lowers to a runtime call. A statepoint-based
  pass can be added later without changing the IR.
  5. Intrinsic lowering (narrow, istype, set ops) consults a small table:
  inline-if-cheap, else call into m3core. This is exactly the policy M3C.m3
  makes implicitly today.
  6. Debug info comes from MSIR source-location metadata, lowered to !dbg and
  DICompileUnit. The existing set_source_file/set_source_line calls feed
  straight in.

  The deliberate property: nothing in lowering needs to look at Target.* except
  for the few items that already vary (word size, EH model, EXTENDED width).
  Everything else is target-neutral.

  ---
  3. Migration path from M3CG

  The cheapest plausible path, in three phases, none of which requires a flag
  day:

  Phase A — MSIR as another M3CG.T. Implement MSIR as a backend that consumes
  M3CG_Ops calls and builds the new IR in memory, then prints/serializes it.
  This is exactly the filter pattern the existing M3CG_Ops interface is built
  for (child: M3CG.T := NIL at line 33; M3CG_Tee, M3CG_DoNothing are precedent).
   No front-end changes; you can diff MSIR-from-M3CG against the C backend's
  output for any package.

  Phase B — Reverse the polarity. Once MSIR is faithful, refactor the front-end
  (m3front, especially stmts/TryStmt.m3, stmts/TryFinStmt.m3, expression
  compilation) to target MSIR directly. Keep M3CG as a legacy backend for m3back
   / m3cc. Now the front-end emits typed, structured IR, and the M3CG adapter
  goes the other way: MSIR → M3CG calls, for the C and gcc backends.

  Phase C — LLVM backend. Add an MSIR-to-LLVM lowering as a peer of the C
  backend. The platform config gains M3_BACKEND_MODE = "LLVM" alongside "C" and
  "3". The two-phase bootstrap protocol from CLAUDE.md already handles "compiler
   with new backend can't compile m3core because m3core uses the new backend's
  runtime" — it's the same dance you'd do here.

  Phase A is the load-bearing one. It's pure addition (new package under
  m3-sys/, no edits to the front-end), and it gives you a checkable artifact
  (MSIR bytecode dumps) before you've committed to any front-end change.

  ---
  What do you want to drill into next? Candidates: (a) MSIR's concrete
  textual/binary form and whether it's worth making it round-trippable, (b) the
  EH lowering in detail since that's where ex_stack/ex_frame currently live, or
  (c) the GC story, which is the riskiest piece because the existing collector
  wasn't designed with statepoints in mind.

⏺ You're right, and I overplayed Phase A. Let me concede properly.

  m3-sys/llvm/llvm22/src/M3CG_LLVM.m3 is a 7,107-line M3CG_Ops consumer that
  emits LLVM bitcode (via the llvm22bindings C bindings). That is Phase A.
  Building another adapter alongside it would be busywork.

  The real question your push-back surfaces is: does an IR above M3CG actually 
  buy anything M3CG doesn't already give you? Let me try to answer that honestly
   by looking at what M3CG keeps vs. loses.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Fair correction. Three things are now clear from looking at the tree:

  1. Phase A already exists. m3-sys/llvm/llvm22/src/M3CG_LLVM.m3 is exactly an
  M3CG_Ops consumer that emits LLVM bitcode through the binding layer — selected
   via M3_BACKEND_MODE="StAloneLlvmAsm" or "StAloneLlvmObj". Building a parallel
   "MSIR-from-M3CG" adapter would be reinventing it.

  2. M3CG is genuinely low-level. Reading M3CG_Ops.i3:
  - Operations are a stack machine over width tags (IType/MType/ZType) — types
  already erased to int/word/float buckets.
  - Layouts are resolved to ByteOffset/BitOffset (e.g. load(v, o, t, u),
  init_int(o, value, t)).
  - No CFG: set_label + jump + if_* + case_jump. The "expression stack must be
  empty at each label" rule (line 463) is the receipt for "this is a stack
  machine, not SSA."
  - Method dispatch is gone — m3front lowers it to indirect call through a slot
  offset before M3CG ever sees it.
  - TRY structure is encoded as ordered Start_try/End_try/Landing_pad calls (the
   same ones whose ordering bit you in TryFinStmt.m3).

  So almost everything I claimed an MSIR-above-M3CG would "preserve" is actually
   decided in m3front, not m3middle. By the time M3CG is called, the information
   is gone.

  3. The real fork is therefore not three phases — it's two genuinely different 
  bets:

  - Bet A: improve M3CG_LLVM. Lower friction, captures ~80% of what a modern
  LLVM backend can give you (decent codegen, real debug info, EH via Itanium
  personality, addrspace-tagged pointers for future GC integration). Doesn't
  need m3front changes. The 7,100 lines are mostly mechanical — there's a lot of
   room to teach it about LLVM types, attributes, and metadata without reshaping
   the architecture.
  - Bet B: surface high-level facts back into the IR. Either add a small set of
  M3CG metadata calls (e.g. "this indirect call is a method dispatch on object
  type T", "this load is from a WITH-bound non-aliasing local", "this range
  check has compile-time-known bounds [lo..hi]"), or do a proper m3front rewrite
   to a typed-SSA IR. This is the only thing that buys you optimizations LLVM
  can't recover from M3CG output: devirtualization, subrange-driven range-check
  elimination, traced-ref aliasing, set-bound folding.

  The MSIR story I told only earns its keep under Bet B, and Bet B has a real
  m3front cost. If the goal is "make CM3 produce good LLVM," Bet A is almost
  certainly the right first move and the existing llvm22 package is where to
  invest.

  What's actually motivating the LLVM retarget for you — codegen quality on
  existing targets, EH/GC modernization, easier porting to new architectures, or
   something else? That changes which bet is right.

❯ Consider the arguments in MSIR.md                                             

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Read it. The framing supersedes my "Phase A" — you're right and MSIR.md is
  right: the only architecture that earns its keep is the one that bypasses M3CG
   entirely, with MSIR fed by m3front and emitted in parallel to (not on top of)
   M3CG. My earlier suggestion was wrong on the boundary.

  A few things I think MSIR.md is correct about, and a few where it's
  underspecified relative to what's actually in this tree.

  Where MSIR.md is right and load-bearing

  - Bypass, don't layer. The information loss happens at the m3front → M3CG
  boundary, not later. Anything sitting downstream of M3CG is reconstructing
  what m3front already knew. M3CG_LLVM.m3 (7,107 lines) is proof: most of its
  bulk is reverse-engineering structure that m3front discarded one call earlier.
  - Keep M3CG for the C and gcc backends. m3back/M3C.m3 has years of subtle
  correctness baked in (the noreturn handling, _M3Exc layout, EXTENDED quirks).
  Don't try to replace it. MSIR ships alongside.
  - Frame it as a new backend, not an evolution. The two-phase bootstrap
  protocol in CLAUDE.md exists precisely for this kind of change.
  - The Swift SIL / Rust MIR analogy is apt. Both languages discovered that LLVM
   IR alone couldn't carry their type discipline; both built a typed-SSA layer
  above. CM3's situation is structurally identical.

  Where the doc is underspecified, with concrete CM3 pointers

  1. The m3front/MSIR boundary needs a real shape. MSIR.md says "extend the
  front end to keep and expose..." — but most of that information is still alive
   in m3front at the point it calls M3CG. The information isn't destroyed; it's
  just not communicated. The two structural choices are:
  - Intercept at the existing call sites. m3front keeps calling something with
  the shape of M3CG_Ops, but MSIR receives a richer set of calls (with type
  UIDs, dispatch slots, EH region IDs intact). This is small surgery and gives
  you a working MVP fast.
  - Split m3front into elaboration + lowering. Cleaner long-term shape, much
  bigger refactor. Probably the right end-state, but not the right MVP.

  I'd start with (1), specifically by adding parallel MSIR-emitting calls inside
   the same m3front traversal that calls M3CG. That gives you cross-validation:
  every package can be compiled both ways, and you can diff outputs.

  2. The EH section understates how much is already done for ex_stack targets.
  ARM64_DARWIN, AMD64_DARWIN, AMD64_LINUX already lower through Itanium-style EH
   — RTStack__ThrowM3Exc, libunwind, the C++ personality, the _M3Exc struct. The
   MSIR work on those targets is mostly not throwing the structure away — it's
  mapping try_except/try_finally MSIR nodes directly to invoke/landingpad/resume
   instead of the brittle Start_try/End_try/Landing_pad ordering hazard in
  TryFinStmt.m3. The hard EH targets are the ex_frame ones, where you'll need to
   keep setjmp/longjmp lowering as a fallback.

  3. The GC story is the riskiest piece and the doc soft-pedals it. CM3's
  collector (m3core/src/runtime/...) is not designed for statepoints. The
  realistic GC progression:
  - MVP: shadow stack via gcroot intrinsics. Correct, slow-ish, lets you use the
   existing collector unchanged.
  - Medium-term: addrspace(1) on traced refs as a forward-compatibility marker;
  still shadow stack underneath.
  - Long-term: statepoint-based precise GC. This is a real collector project,
  not a backend project. Don't put it on the MSIR critical path.

  4. The doc doesn't mention bootstrap, and bootstrap is half the work. Adopting
   MSIR as a canonical backend means: m3core itself eventually needs to be
  compiled by the MSIR path. Until that's true, you ship as a non-default
  backend (M3_BACKEND_MODE = "MSIR" alongside "C"/"3"/"StAloneLlvmAsm"). The
  first-class adoption requires the same dance documented in CLAUDE.md's
  "Two-Phase Bootstrap" section.

  5. The cost estimate is for the whole end-state. 12–24 months for a
  Swift-SIL-equivalent typed-SSA IR with custom passes is right, but a useful
  MVP — single platform, single backend mode, language subset enough to compile
  a small program with TRY/EXCEPT and an OBJECT — is more like 3–4 months. The
  way to keep the project tractable is to commit early to what's out of scope 
  for MVP: no custom optimization passes, no statepoint GC, no bootstrap
  adoption, no replacement of M3CG_LLVM. Just: "MSIR exists, it's typed, it
  lowers a defined subset, its output runs."

  A concrete MVP shape

  If I were the one starting this Monday, I'd aim for:

  1. New package m3-sys/msir containing the IR types and a printer.
  2. m3front gains an MSIR.T builder that runs in parallel with the existing
  M3CG.T calls — same traversal, two outputs. Gated by a build flag so it's
  invisible until you ask for it.
  3. New package m3-sys/msir2llvm that consumes MSIR and emits LLVM bitcode
  (reusing m3-sys/llvm/llvm22bindings).
  4. New M3_BACKEND_MODE = "MSIR" in one platform config (ARM64_DARWIN — it's
  the most modern, has ex_stack, and doesn't need _Float128).
  5. Test scope: compile a single hand-written .m3 file with TRY/EXCEPT, an
  OBJECT with one method, and an open-array parameter. Run it. Compare against
  the C-backend output for behavior.

  Items 1–3 are pure addition: nothing existing breaks. Item 4 is one config
  edit. Item 5 is the falsification target — if it works, the architecture is
  validated; if it doesn't, you've learned something specific and cheap.

  One thing worth deciding before code

  The most consequential design decision is what the MSIR type system asserts 
  about pointers. Specifically: do traced refs get a distinct LLVM address space
   from day one? Saying yes costs almost nothing now (LLVM treats addrspace(1)
  the same as addrspace(0) on most targets) and preserves every future GC
  option. Saying no — flat pointers, GC-ness in metadata only — closes off
  statepoint integration as a forklift upgrade later. I'd say yes; the doc is
  silent on this and it's the kind of decision that's expensive to revisit.

  ---
  Want me to save the MSIR direction to project memory (with a pointer to
  MSIR.md as the design doc), and/or sketch the concrete shape of the MSIR types
   and the m3front/MSIR builder interface?

❯ Some things to add: the current collector is not non-moving. It is            
  conservative on the stacks but mostly-copying for heap objects that are not   
  referenced from the stacks. So, we are not so far off being able consider gc  
  statepoints.                                                                  

  Searched for 1 pattern, listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That changes the picture meaningfully. Confirmed in the source —
  RTCollector.i3:117 describes it explicitly: "conservative mostly-copying
  collector that uses VM protection on...". This is Bartlett-style
  ambiguous-roots, with the SRC incremental refinement (Appel/Ellis/Li read
  barrier via VM protection). My characterization of statepoint GC as
  "long-term, riskiest piece" was wrong for this collector specifically.

  The substrate that statepoints actually need is precise tracing of 
  heap-internal references, and you already have it — every traced object has
  its trace map (RTHeapMap), every heap-to-heap edge is precise, and the
  evacuation machinery is live. The only ambiguity is stack roots, and
  Bartlett's whole point is that ambiguous stack roots don't preclude copying;
  they just pin the pages they point into.

  So the GC progression looks much gentler than I described:

  - MVP under MSIR-LLVM: keep the ambiguous-stack model as-is. Emit traced refs
  in addrspace(1). Stack scanning continues to use the existing conservative
  walk (libunwind + scan-as-integer). Heap loads/stores go through MSIR
  gc.load/gc.store ops that lower to plain load/store plus the existing read
  barrier (the VM protection handles it transparently anyway). Write barriers —
  to the extent the current generational/incremental machinery uses them —
  become explicit MSIR ops rather than being hand-emitted by the front-end.
  - Refinement: statepoints become an opt-in refinement — initially for hot
  procedures, eventually default. They let you precise-track traced values
  across calls within the SSA region, taking those refs off the dependency on
  the conservative stack scan. Pages whose only roots are now-precise refs
  become fully copy-eligible, which trades pin-induced fragmentation for a small
   per-call statepoint cost. Bartlett-with-some-precise-roots is a coherent
  intermediate; you don't need a flag day.
  - End-state: full precise stacks via statepoints, with the conservative scan
  retained only as a safety net for foreign frames (C calls, libraries). The
  collector's heap machinery doesn't need to change at all — that's the part
  that's already precise.

  This makes the addrspace(1) decision more obviously correct, not less. It
  gives the LLVM optimizer the alias separation between traced and untraced from
   day one, and it makes the eventual statepoint pass a drop-in transformation
  rather than a re-typing exercise.

  One caveat worth naming: the VM-protection read barrier interacts with
  statepoint placement. Statepoints assume pointer values can be relocated at
  safepoints; the read barrier assumes that loading from a protected page traps
  and that the trap handler does the forwarding. As long as MSIR's gc.load
  lowers consistently (either through the barrier, or through a statepoint-aware
   reload of the relocated pointer), these compose. But it's the kind of seam
  that would benefit from being designed in from the start rather than bolted on
   — i.e., MSIR should distinguish "load through possibly-forwarded ref" from
  "raw memory load" as different ops.

  Let me save the relevant project context so this doesn't get re-litigated next
   time.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 54s

❯ Note also that the current collector can run without VM protection as there   
  are barriers compiled into the code already that check the state of           
  references loaded from the heap and forwards them if they are in from-space.  
  The thread stacks maintain a to-space invariant from the beginning of GC      
  during stop-the-world thread scanning.                                        

⏺ Good — that's a stronger property than the VM-protection mode alone, and it
  changes the MSIR contract.

  The two-mode reading: there's a software-checked read barrier compiled into
  reference loads (check forwarding, redirect to to-space if in from-space), and
   there's the VM-protection mode as an alternative fast path. The mutator-side
  invariant — thread stacks are to-space from the start of GC, established by 
  stop-the-world stack scanning — means the conservative roots are not just
  ambiguous pointers but ambiguous to-space pointers throughout the mutator
  phase.

  Three consequences for MSIR:

  1. gc.load has a textbook-clean LLVM lowering. Load the slot, test the
  forwarding marker, branch to a cold slow-path that calls into the runtime to
  forward and update the slot, otherwise use the value. That's three or four
  LLVM instructions plus a cold call edge — all of which the optimizer is
  already excellent at: CSE across barriers, hoist out of loops when the slot is
   loop-invariant, fold when the source is provably in to-space (e.g.
  just-allocated), inline the fast path at -O2. None of this depends on signals,
   page protection, or platform-specific machinery. So the MSIR contract should
  be defined against the software barrier model; VM protection becomes an
  implementation choice the runtime can take or leave, invisible to the IR.

  2. The "possibly-forwarded vs raw" distinction I floated earlier collapses 
  into something simpler. With the to-space stack invariant, the rule is just:
  every load through a heap-resident traced slot goes through gc.load; loads 
  from already-in-register traced values are plain LLVM loads of the underlying 
  type. No special "raw memory load" op needed. The barrier lives at the slot
  read, not at every use of the value. That's also what lets LLVM hoist usefully
   — once you have a register-resident traced ref, subsequent uses are
  unguarded.

  3. Statepoint integration becomes almost trivial in this model. Between
  safepoints, the mutator preserves the to-space invariant on stacks via the
  software barrier. At a safepoint, evacuation may relocate refs the mutator
  holds in SSA. Statepoints communicate exactly that: the relocation set. With
  the existing barrier discipline, the statepoint pass doesn't need a redesign
  of the runtime's view of stack roots — it just needs to be allowed to update
  SSA-resident refs to their new addresses. The conservative-stack code path
  stays untouched for foreign frames and for the bootstrapping window before
  stack-precise emission is universal.

  One MSIR-shape decision this nails down: traced-ref slot writes are also their
   own op (gc.store), because under generational/incremental policies they need
  write-barrier emission too — remembered-set updates, card marking, whatever
  the collector wants. Keeping gc.store as a distinct op (not just store on an
  addrspace(1) value) gives the lowering pass a single hook to add or remove
  that machinery without re-walking IR.

  Let me update the collector memory so this isn't lost.

  Wrote 1 memory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Memory updated with the two-mode barrier and the to-space-stack-invariant. The
   MSIR contract now sits cleanly on the software-barrier model, with VM
  protection as a runtime implementation choice below the IR — which is the
  right separation regardless of what we choose to ship first.
