❯ Further: Great — I’ll do all three, in a **single coherent narrative**, so    
  they reinforce each other rather than repeating concepts.                     
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  # 1. Sketch of a Minimal *Modula‑3 Semantic IR* (MSIR)                        
                                                                                
  The goal of a Modula‑3 Semantic IR (let’s call it **MSIR**) is **not** to     
  replace LLVM, but to sit *above* LLVM while *below* the AST. Its purpose is   
  to preserve **language meaning** long enough to map it intentionally onto     
  LLVM IR.                                                                      
                                                                                
  Think of it as *“the last place where Modula‑3 still looks like Modula‑3.”*   
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 1.1 Design principles                                                      
                                                                                
  MSIR should be:                                                               
                                                                                
  *   **Typed** (stronger than LLVM, weaker than AST)                           
  *   **Structured** (explicit constructs, not encoded control flow)            
  *   **Stable** (not tied to LLVM version churn)                               
  *   **Lowerable** (every node has a deterministic LLVM mapping)               
                                                                                
  Avoid:                                                                        
                                                                                
  *   AST trivia (syntax)                                                       
  *   Backend artifacts (stack offsets, concrete layouts)                       
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 1.2 Core entities in MSIR                                                  
                                                                                
  ### Modules and interfaces                                                    
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  module M                                                                      
    exports I1, I2                                                              
    imports N, O                                                                
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  *   Preserve compilation units                                                
  *   Explicit import/export graph                                              
  *   Enables whole‑program and link‑time reasoning                             
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Types (identity‑preserving)                                               
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  type T = OBJECT                                                               
    METHODS                                                                     
      m(x: INTEGER): INTEGER                                                    
    END                                                                         
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  Key properties:                                                               
                                                                                
  *   Type identity is *stable* and *named*                                     
  *   Layout is separate from identity                                          
  *   Allows RTTI, NARROW, TYPECASE without reconstruction                      
                                                                                
  Internally:                                                                   
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  TypeDecl {                                                                    
    id: TypeID                                                                  
    kind: Object | Ref | Array | Record | ...                                   
    super: TypeID?                                                              
  }                                                                             
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Objects and methods                                                       
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  method T.m(this: REF T, x: INTEGER): INTEGER                                  
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  *   Method identity explicit                                                  
  *   `this` distinguished semantically                                         
  *   Override chains preserved                                                 
                                                                                
  Dispatch is *not* yet compiled into function pointers — that happens later.   
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Procedures                                                                
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  proc P(a: INTEGER): INTEGER                                                   
    locals b: INTEGER                                                           
    body:                                                                       
      RETURN a + b                                                              
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  Key characteristics:                                                          
                                                                                
  *   Structured control flow                                                   
  *   Lexical nesting preserved (for inner procedures)                          
  *   Clear root lifetimes for GC                                               
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Control flow (structured)                                                 
                                                                                
  Instead of basic blocks immediately:                                          
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  IF cond THEN                                                                  
    ...                                                                         
  ELSE                                                                          
    ...                                                                         
  END                                                                           
                                                                                
  TRY                                                                           
    ...                                                                         
  EXCEPT E1 => ...                                                              
  END                                                                           
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  This postpones irreversibility.                                               
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Exceptions                                                                
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  exception E                                                                   
  RAISE E                                                                       
  TRY ... EXCEPT E => ...                                                       
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  *   Exception types are first‑class                                           
  *   No lowering to return codes or runtime calls                              
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Garbage‑collected references                                              
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  gc_ref T                                                                      
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  *   Every GC‑managed value is explicitly marked                               
  *   Escape and lifetime info remains visible                                  
  *   Enables *precise* GC lowering                                             
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Open arrays                                                               
                                                                                
  ```text                                                                       
  open_array[T] { data, length, shape }                                         
  ```                                                                           
                                                                                
  Semantics preserved:                                                          
                                                                                
  *   Bounds                                                                    
  *   Rank                                                                      
  *   Passing conventions                                                       
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 1.3 What MSIR deliberately omits                                           
                                                                                
  *   Stack layout                                                              
  *   Calling convention details                                                
  *   Instruction selection                                                     
  *   ABI specifics                                                             
                                                                                
  Those are LLVM’s job.                                                         
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  # 2. How Rust and Swift Solved the Same Problem                               
                                                                                
  CM3 is not alone here — this problem has already been solved *twice* in       
  modern compiler design.                                                       
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 2.1 Rust: HIR → MIR → LLVM IR                                              
                                                                                
  Rust initially tried near‑direct lowering to LLVM and hit the **same wall**   
  CM3 is hitting now.                                                           
                                                                                
  ### Rust’s solution:                                                          
                                                                                
      AST                                                                       
        → HIR (semantic, name‑resolved)                                         
          → MIR (typed, control‑flow normalized)                                
            → LLVM IR                                                           
                                                                                
  Key lessons:                                                                  
                                                                                
  *   LLVM is *too low‑level* for ownership, lifetimes, and panics              
  *   Semantic invariants must survive optimization                             
  *   MIR is the “semantic firewall” that prevents LLVM from breaking language  
  rules                                                                         
                                                                                
  Direct analogy:                                                               
                                                                                
  *   **MSIR ≈ Rust MIR**                                                       
  *   M3CG ≈ “what Rust explicitly avoided”                                     
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 2.2 Swift: AST → SIL → LLVM IR                                             
                                                                                
  Swift faced:                                                                  
                                                                                
  *   ARC                                                                       
  *   Exceptions                                                                
  *   Protocols (interfaces)                                                    
  *   Value semantics + references                                              
                                                                                
  Swift introduced **SIL (Swift Intermediate Language)**.                       
                                                                                
  SIL:                                                                          
                                                                                
  *   Is typed                                                                  
  *   Has ownership and lifetime rules                                          
  *   Models method dispatch explicitly                                         
  *   Is optimized *before* lowering to LLVM                                    
                                                                                
  Direct analogy:                                                               
                                                                                
  *   **MSIR ≈ SIL**                                                            
  *   Objective‑C interop similar in complexity to Modula‑3’s GC + objects      
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 2.3 Key shared insight                                                     
                                                                                
  > **LLVM IR is not a semantic IR. It is a lowering target.**                  
                                                                                
  Both Rust and Swift only became viable once they accepted this.               
                                                                                
  CM3 has not yet crossed that threshold.                                       
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  # 3. A Practical Migration Plan for CM3                                       
                                                                                
  This must be **incremental** and **non‑disruptive**, or it will fail.         
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 0: Freeze M3CG semantics                                             
                                                                                
  *   Declare M3CG “legacy‑backend IR”                                          
  *   Do not evolve it further                                                  
  *   Treat it as an implementation detail                                      
                                                                                
  This avoids fighting two wars at once.                                        
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 1: Introduce MSIR *in parallel*                                      
                                                                                
      Front end                                                                 
        → Semantic analysis                                                     
          → MSIR                                                                
          → LLVM IR        (new path)                                           
          → M3CG → C/GCC   (existing)                                           
                                                                                
  Critical rule:                                                                
                                                                                
  > **Do not emit M3CG first. MSIR must be primary.**                           
                                                                                
  Initially:                                                                    
                                                                                
  *   Only integers                                                             
  *   Only procedures                                                           
  *   No exceptions                                                             
  *   No objects                                                                
                                                                                
  Goal: correctness, not completeness.                                          
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 2: Add GC correctness                                                
                                                                                
  This is the make‑or‑break step.                                               
                                                                                
  *   Make GC roots explicit in MSIR                                            
  *   Choose LLVM lowering strategy:                                            
      *   Statepoint‑based GC (preferred long‑term)                             
      *   Shadow stack (simpler initially)                                      
                                                                                
  Once GC is correct, LLVM becomes viable.                                      
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 3: Objects, methods, interfaces                                      
                                                                                
  *   Emit vtables explicitly                                                   
  *   Preserve type descriptors                                                 
  *   Lower dispatch late                                                       
                                                                                
  This enables:                                                                 
                                                                                
  *   Devirtualization                                                          
  *   Better inlining                                                           
  *   Real debug info                                                           
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 4: Exceptions and control flow                                       
                                                                                
  *   Map `TRY/EXCEPT` → `invoke`/`landingpad`                                  
  *   Preserve exception types through LLVM                                     
  *   Avoid “exceptions as gotos”                                               
                                                                                
  At this point LLVM optimizations stop fighting you.                           
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Phase 5: Optimization and tooling                                          
                                                                                
  Only now:                                                                     
                                                                                
  *   Add Modula‑3‑aware LLVM passes                                            
  *   Add sanitizer support                                                     
  *   Add proper DWARF mappings                                                 
  *   Enable LTO                                                                
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## 4. Cost, risk, and payoff                                                  
                                                                                
  ### Cost                                                                      
                                                                                
  *   \~1–2 experienced compiler engineers                                      
  *   \~12–24 months to production quality                                      
  *   Ongoing LLVM version maintenance                                          
                                                                                
  ### Risks                                                                     
                                                                                
  *   GC integration complexity                                                 
  *   LLVM API churn                                                            
  *   Partial backend during transition                                         
                                                                                
  ### Payoff                                                                    
                                                                                
  ✅ Correct semantics                                                          
  ✅ Modern optimization                                                        
  ✅ First‑class debugging                                                      
  ✅ Cross‑platform viability                                                   
  ✅ Future‑proof backend                                                       
                                                                                
  ***                                                                           
                                                                                
  ## Final takeaway                                                             
                                                                                
  What you are proposing is **exactly the transition CM3 must make** if LLVM is 
   to be anything more than an experiment.                                      
                                                                                
  You are not “making LLVM better”; you are **making Modula‑3 compilable in a   
  modern world**.                                                               
                                                                                
  If you want, next steps could be:                                             
                                                                                
  *   a concrete MSIR grammar                                                   
  *   a LLVM‑lowering example for one construct (e.g. OBJECT + METHOD)          
  *   or a “what would I prototype first in 3 months” plan                      
                                                                                
⏺ The expanded version is stronger than the original MSIR.md — particularly
  Phase 0 ("freeze M3CG semantics") and the Rust/Swift comparison. A few
  targeted reactions, then I'd pick OBJECT+METHOD as the next concrete step.

  Where the expanded narrative is right

  - Phase 0 is the most important addition. Not because M3CG should stop being
  maintained — m3back/M3C.m3 and the gcc backend will need bug fixes for years —
   but because its op surface should stop evolving. New language facts go
  through MSIR; M3CG becomes a frozen contract from m3front to the legacy
  backends. This prevents the failure mode where MSIR and M3CG drift in parallel
   and m3front has to choose-and-double-emit forever.
  - The Rust/Swift framing is correct that LLVM IR is a lowering target, not a 
  semantic IR. Both projects discovered this the hard way; CM3 doesn't need to.
  - The migration phases' ordering — IR first, GC correctness second, objects
  third, EH fourth, optimization last — is the right dependency graph. Trying to
   do EH before GC is a common mistake and this ordering avoids it.

  Where I'd push back or refine

  1. MSIR is closer to MIR than to SIL. Swift SIL carries ownership, ARC
  retain/release, copy-on-write value semantics, and protocol witness tables.
  Modula‑3 has none of these — it's a traced-ref language with no ownership
  beyond GC, no value-type COW, no protocol witnesses. Rust MIR's intent
  (typed-SSA, drop tracking, panic regions, LLVM-shaped CFG) is the closer fit.
  The honest analogy is: MSIR is to Modula‑3 what JVM bytecode is to Java —
  typed, dispatch-aware, EH-aware, GC-aware, but not ownership-aware. That makes
   MSIR meaningfully simpler than SIL or MIR, which is good news for the cost
  estimate.

  2. "Structured control flow" needs to be split. Keeping IF/THEN/ELSE and WHILE
   as nested constructs in MSIR has a real cost — every analysis pass has to
  walk nesting — for almost no benefit, since LLVM wants a CFG anyway. Keeping
  TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY structural is genuinely load-bearing, because that's where
  the Start_try/End_try ordering hazard from TryFinStmt.m3 lives. The right
  split:
  - CFG of basic blocks with block parameters for ordinary control flow. Cheap
  to emit, trivial to lower (block params → phis), what every optimization pass
  expects.
  - Structured EH regions as outer envelopes around CFG fragments. Lowered to
  invoke/landingpad/resume (or setjmp) at the very end.

  This is what MIR and SIL both do, and it's what avoids the "EH-as-gotos"
  failure the doc rightly warns about.

  3. The MSIR sketch is missing the GC-load/GC-store distinction we just 
  established. With the software-barrier model and the to-space stack invariant,
   gc.load and gc.store need to be distinct ops, not just load/store on a gc_ref
   T type. The doc shows gc_ref T as a type marker (good) but doesn't make slot
  reads vs. register-resident uses syntactically different (needed). Otherwise
  the lowering pass can't tell whether to emit a barrier check.

  4. The MSIR sketch is also missing M3-distinctive intrinsics that should be 
  first-class. Specifically: narrow, istype, typecase (for OBJECT
  discrimination), set_union/set_intersect/set_member, range_check lo hi,
  nil_check, subscript_check, case_select. These are exactly the ops where
  preserving language meaning pays off in optimization — range_check on a
  constant range disappears, istype on a known concrete type devirtualizes,
  set_member on a small set folds to and+icmp. If they get lowered to runtime
  calls in MSIR, LLVM can't see through them.

  5. Phase 1's MVP scope is too narrow to be useful as a falsification target.
  "Only integers / no procedures-with-locals / no objects / no exceptions"
  compiles essentially nothing real. The smallest scope that proves the
  architecture is:
  - procedures with locals, parameters, and return
  - one OBJECT type with one method (forces dispatch + RTTI + traced ref)
  - one TRY/EXCEPT (forces the EH lowering decision)
  - one open array parameter (forces the dope-vector ABI)

  That's still maybe 3 months of work, but the result actually exercises every
  load-bearing seam in the architecture. The "integers only" MVP can ship in a
  week and tells you nothing.

  6. The 12–24 month estimate is for full Phase 5. It bundles together "minimum
  useful" and "production quality with custom passes and LTO" into one number,
  which makes the project look unfundable. A more honest decomposition: ~3
  months to Phase 1 (parallel path, useful subset compiles); ~9 months to end of
   Phase 4 (objects + EH + GC correct); the remaining 12–15 months are Phase 5
  polish that doesn't block usability.

  One thing the doc should commit to that it currently dodges

  The doc says "Choose LLVM lowering strategy: Statepoint-based GC (preferred
  long-term) / Shadow stack (simpler initially)." Given what we now know about
  CM3's collector — software-checked barrier, to-space stack invariant — neither
   of those is quite the right framing. The actual MVP strategy is "do nothing":
   keep the existing conservative stack scan, emit addrspace(1) traced refs,
  lower gc.load/gc.store to inline barrier checks, let the existing collector
  treat SSA-resident traced refs as ambiguous stack roots when they spill. No
  shadow stack, no statepoints. Statepoints become a later optimization that
  takes specific refs off the conservative-scan dependency. The doc currently
  overstates the GC engineering cost.

  What to do next

  Of your three offers, the OBJECT + METHOD lowering walkthrough forces the most
   decisions per page: it touches the type system (object identity, super
  chains), RTTI (TYPECASE), dispatch (vtable layout, method selectors), GC
  (objects are traced, this is a gc_ref), the ABI (how this is passed), and the
  LLVM mapping (struct layout, vtable globals, indirect call) all at once. If we
   pick that, we'll discover what MSIR's type-system grammar actually has to
  look like, rather than describing it abstractly.

  Want me to do that walkthrough? End-to-end: source M3 fragment → MSIR → LLVM
  IR for one small object type with two methods, one inherited and one
  overridden, allocated, dispatched, narrowed, and freed-by-GC.

✻ Worked for 1m 20s

❯ Let's do that walk-through                                                    

⏺ Walkthrough below. I've picked the smallest source fragment that exercises
  both an inherited method and an overridden one, plus allocation, dispatch,
  field access, subtyping, and a global traced slot. Skipping TRY/EXCEPT and
  open arrays — those are different walkthroughs.

  ---
  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  TYPE
    Shape = OBJECT
      METHODS
        area(): REAL    := DefaultArea;
        kind(): INTEGER := DefaultKind;
      END;

    Circle = Shape OBJECT
      r: REAL;
    OVERRIDES
      area := CircleArea;
    END;

  PROCEDURE DefaultArea (self: Shape):  REAL    = BEGIN RETURN 0.0 END
  DefaultArea;
  PROCEDURE DefaultKind (self: Shape):  INTEGER = BEGIN RETURN 0   END
  DefaultKind;
  PROCEDURE CircleArea  (self: Circle): REAL    = BEGIN RETURN 3.14159 * self.r
  * self.r END CircleArea;

  PROCEDURE Sum (s: Shape): REAL =
    BEGIN
      RETURN s.area() + FLOAT(s.kind());
    END Sum;

  VAR c: Shape;
  BEGIN
    c := NEW(Circle, r := 2.0);
    EVAL Sum(c);
  END Demo.

  What this exercises: virtual dispatch on a known-method selector (area,
  overridden), virtual dispatch on an inherited slot (kind, not overridden),
  heap allocation of a traced object, write to a module-global traced slot,
  upcast Circle <: Shape at parameter passing, field access on a subclass
  through its concrete view.

  ---
  2. MSIR

  I'm presenting a textual sketch of the MSIR I have in mind. SSA-style values,
  block parameters, structured EH envelopes (none in this fragment), but flat
  CFG for ordinary control. M3-distinctive ops (dispatch, new, narrow, gc.load,
  gc.store) are first-class.

  module Demo
    exports {Demo}

    ; ---- type declarations -------------------------------------------------

    type Shape = object {
      super:    <root>
      fields:   { }                                    ; (header is implicit)
      methods:  [ area: proc(gc_ref Shape)  -> f64,
                  kind: proc(gc_ref Shape)  -> i64 ]
      defaults: [ area := @DefaultArea,
                  kind := @DefaultKind ]
      descriptor: @Shape_ti
    }

    type Circle = object {
      super:    Shape
      fields:   { r: f64 }                             ; appended after Shape
      methods:  [ area: <inherited slot 0, overridden>,
                  kind: <inherited slot 1, default> ]
      overrides:[ area := @CircleArea ]
      descriptor: @Circle_ti
    }

    ; ---- procedures --------------------------------------------------------

    proc @DefaultArea(self: gc_ref Shape) -> f64
      entry:
        ret 0.0

    proc @DefaultKind(self: gc_ref Shape) -> i64
      entry:
        ret 0

    proc @CircleArea(self: gc_ref Circle) -> f64
      entry:
        %r   = field_load self, Circle.r              ; non-traced field, plain
  load
        %sq  = fmul %r, %r
        %res = fmul 3.14159, %sq
        ret %res

    proc @Sum(s: gc_ref Shape) -> f64
      entry:
        %a    = dispatch s, Shape.area     -> f64     ; virtual call
        %k.i  = dispatch s, Shape.kind     -> i64     ; virtual call (inherited)
        %k.f  = sitofp %k.i to f64
        %sum  = fadd %a, %k.f
        ret %sum

    ; ---- module init -------------------------------------------------------

    global @Demo.c : gc_slot Shape                    ; traced global (root)

    proc @Demo__init() -> void
      entry:
        %c0 = new Circle                              ; traced alloc; header set
        field_store %c0, Circle.r, 2.0
        gc.store @Demo.c, %c0                         ; write barrier + slot
  store
        %s  = gc.load  @Demo.c                        ; barrier-checked slot
  read
        call @Sum(%s) -> _
        ret

  Things worth noticing about the IR shape:

  - dispatch s, Shape.area is a single op. The selector is named, not encoded as
   a slot index. The lowering pass assigns slot offsets per type once it has the
   full method table. Devirtualization at MSIR level becomes "rewrite dispatch
  to direct call when the static type pins the method."
  - gc_ref T vs gc_slot T: register-resident traced ref vs heap/global-resident
  traced slot. gc.load operates on a slot; the result is a gc_ref. Once you have
   a gc_ref in SSA, field_load of a non-traced field is unguarded.
  - Type descriptors are MSIR-level entities, not opaque blobs. The lowering
  pass synthesizes @Shape_ti/@Circle_ti as LLVM globals from the descriptor
  slot.
  - new Circle carries the type identity, not a size. The runtime allocator gets
   a descriptor and figures out the size from the descriptor's tracemap. This is
   exactly what the existing RTAllocator interface wants.
  - Upcast is invisible. gc.store @Demo.c, %c0 stores a gc_ref Circle into a
  gc_slot Shape. The MSIR type system accepts that as a width-subtyping
  admissible store; lowering emits no coercion code.

  ---
  3. LLVM IR

  Lowering pass output. Comments mark where each MSIR op went.

  target datalayout = "..."

  ; ---- type-descriptor layout (RTType) ------------------------------------

  ; { super, typecode, tracemap, n_methods, methods... }
  %RTType = type { ptr, i32, ptr, i32, [0 x ptr] }

  ; ---- object layouts -----------------------------------------------------

  %Shape  = type { ptr }              ; { typeinfo }
  %Circle = type { ptr, double }      ; { typeinfo, r }   ; Shape's prefix at 
  offset 0

  ; ---- type descriptors as constants --------------------------------------

  @Shape_tracemap  = external constant i8           ; emitted by GC layout pass
  @Circle_tracemap = external constant i8

  ; Shape's method table: [area, kind]
  @Shape_ti = constant { ptr, i32, ptr, i32, [2 x ptr] } {
    ptr null,                                       ; super
    i32 1,                                          ; typecode
    ptr @Shape_tracemap,
    i32 2,
    [2 x ptr] [ ptr @DefaultArea, ptr @DefaultKind ]
  }

  ; Circle's method table: [CircleArea (overridden), DefaultKind (inherited)]
  @Circle_ti = constant { ptr, i32, ptr, i32, [2 x ptr] } {
    ptr @Shape_ti,                                  ; super
    i32 2,                                          ; typecode
    ptr @Circle_tracemap,
    i32 2,
    [2 x ptr] [ ptr @CircleArea, ptr @DefaultKind ]
  }

  ; ---- procedures ---------------------------------------------------------

  define double @DefaultArea(ptr addrspace(1) %self) {
    ret double 0.0
  }

  define i64 @DefaultKind(ptr addrspace(1) %self) {
    ret i64 0
  }

  define double @CircleArea(ptr addrspace(1) %self) {
    ; field_load self.r — non-traced field; plain load through gc_ref
    %r.p = getelementptr %Circle, ptr addrspace(1) %self, i32 0, i32 1
    %r   = load double, ptr addrspace(1) %r.p
    %sq  = fmul double %r, %r
    %res = fmul double 0x400921F9F01B866E, %sq      ; 3.14159
    ret double %res
  }

  ; helper macro-equivalent: load a method pointer from object's typecell
  ;   %ti  = load typeinfo from header
  ;   %m.p = gep into method table at slot N
  ;   %m   = load method ptr
  ;   indirect call

  define double @Sum(ptr addrspace(1) %s) {
    ; ----- dispatch s, Shape.area  (slot 0)
    ; header is at offset 0; the typecell pointer it holds is to an immutable
    ; global, NOT a traced ref — so this is a plain load, not gc.load.
    %hdr.p   = getelementptr %Shape, ptr addrspace(1) %s, i32 0, i32 0
    %ti      = load ptr, ptr addrspace(1) %hdr.p, !invariant.load !0
    %tbl.p   = getelementptr %RTType, ptr %ti, i32 0, i32 4
    %area.p  = getelementptr [0 x ptr], ptr %tbl.p, i32 0, i32 0
    %area    = load ptr, ptr %area.p, !invariant.load !0
    %a       = call double %area(ptr addrspace(1) %s)

    ; ----- dispatch s, Shape.kind  (slot 1) — typeinfo already loaded, reuse
    %kind.p  = getelementptr [0 x ptr], ptr %tbl.p, i32 0, i32 1
    %kind    = load ptr, ptr %kind.p, !invariant.load !0
    %ki      = call i64 %kind(ptr addrspace(1) %s)

    ; ----- sitofp + fadd
    %kf      = sitofp i64 %ki to double
    %sum     = fadd double %a, %kf
    ret double %sum
  }

  ; ---- module init --------------------------------------------------------

  @Demo.c = global ptr addrspace(1) null

  ; Registered as a GC root by the linker pass via a side-table emission.
  @llvm.used = appending global [1 x ptr] [ ptr @Demo.c ], section
  "llvm.metadata"

  declare ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr)
  declare void @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr, ptr addrspace(1))
  declare ptr addrspace(1) @RTHeap__GcLoad(ptr)     ; or inlined; see below

  define void @Demo__init() {
    ; new Circle: runtime allocator returns zero-initialized object with header 
  set
    %c0   = call ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr @Circle_ti)

    ; field_store c0.r := 2.0
    %r.p  = getelementptr %Circle, ptr addrspace(1) %c0, i32 0, i32 1
    store double 2.0e+0, ptr addrspace(1) %r.p

    ; gc.store @Demo.c, %c0:
    ;   - global slot is "old gen" by virtue of being a global; needs write 
  barrier
    ;   - barrier call records cross-generational edge if any
    call void @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr @Demo.c, ptr addrspace(1) %c0)
    store ptr addrspace(1) %c0, ptr @Demo.c

    ; gc.load @Demo.c — barrier-checked load.
    ; Inlined fast-path form (LLVM hoists/folds it well):
    %slot   = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr @Demo.c
    %fwd    = call ptr addrspace(1) @RTHeap__BarrierCheck(ptr addrspace(1)
  %slot)
    ; (alternative: inline the from-space test against the heap's bounds, only
    ;  call the runtime on a miss. Same semantics.)

    call double @Sum(ptr addrspace(1) %fwd)
    ret void
  }

  !0 = !{}     ; invariant.load: typecell pointers and method tables never 
  change

  Annotations on what survived and what was forged:

  - !invariant.load on the typecell load and on every method-table slot load.
  CM3 type descriptors are immutable globals, so LLVM is allowed to CSE these
  aggressively across calls and across loops. This is one of the wins
  MSIR-via-LLVM gets that the C-via-clang path misses (clang has no way to
  know).
  - The two dispatch ops in Sum share a single typecell load. That sharing is
  not an MSIR-level optimization — MSIR emitted two dispatch ops — it's the
  lowering pass noticing that both selectors target the same object. This is the
   right factoring: keep MSIR clean, keep optimization in the lowering pass.
  - addrspace(1) appears on every traced ref. LLVM treats it as a distinct alias
   domain by default; passes that don't understand the GC won't break it.
  - @llvm.used entry for @Demo.c is the GC-root registration. The real lowering
  would emit a structured side-table the runtime walks at startup; this is the
  pedagogical version.
  - Allocator signature is (typeinfo) -> gc_ref. Size and tracemap come from the
   typeinfo. This matches RTAllocator__New style.
  - Write barrier for the global slot: shown as a runtime call. If the collector
   has a card-marking fast path, this can be inlined.

  ---
  4. Decisions this walkthrough forces

  Six concrete choices fall out, each of which would otherwise have been waved
  at:

  1. Object header is one word holding a pointer to an immutable typecell. Not
  two words, not a fat pointer. The typecell is reachable via that single load
  and supports both dispatch and RTTI.
  2. Methods are stored in the typecell's table, not inline at the front of the 
  object. Less spatial locality but smaller objects, simpler subclass layout,
  and the !invariant.load trick recovers most of the locality cost.
  3. Method-table slot assignment is the IR lowering pass's job, not MSIR's.
  MSIR uses named selectors (Shape.area); lowering computes a stable slot order
  across the inheritance chain. This means devirtualization can happen at MSIR
  level (rewrite dispatch to call when the type pins the method) without
  committing to slot numbers.
  4. Subclass layout is prefix-extension. %Circle has %Shape's fields at offset
  0, then its own. Upcast is a no-op at LLVM level.
  5. Typecell pointer is not a traced ref. Header reads are plain loads, not
  gc.load. This is the first place where the slot-vs-register distinction we
  worked out earns its keep — without it, every dispatch would have a barrier
  check on the header read.
  6. Globals holding traced refs need both a write barrier on store and root 
  registration with the runtime. Both are implicit in MSIR (gc.store and gc_slot
   T); both become explicit at lowering. Stack-resident traced refs need
  neither, because the conservative scan finds them.

  ---
  5. What's not yet decided

  This walkthrough deliberately ducked three things, each of which is its own
  walkthrough:

  - NARROW / TYPECASE. Super-chain walk vs Schubert numbering vs Cohen's
  algorithm. Different cost/binary-size tradeoffs. The simple chain walk works
  for MVP.
  - Open-array dope vector ABI. How ARRAY OF ARRAY OF T lowers, who allocates
  the dope, how subarray slicing works.
  - TRY/EXCEPT lowering against _M3Exc and the existing C++ personality. This is
   where the MSIR structured-EH envelope earns its keep over the M3CG
  Start_try/End_try model.

  If we did open arrays next, the type system additions are concentrated
  (openarray T is a structural type with rank metadata); if we did TRY/EXCEPT
  next, the EH region nesting and landing-pad threading are concentrated. Either
   is a productive next step. I'd lean toward TRY/EXCEPT — it's the higher-risk
  piece, and the open-array story is mostly a matter of typing the dope vector.

  Want me to continue with TRY/EXCEPT next, or pause and turn what we have into
  a concrete MSIR grammar / type spec?

✻ Baked for 2m 59s

❯ Just an aside: could we continue to take advantage of the typemaps and gcmaps 
   currently generated by m3front to retain RTTI or does that break with        
  LLVM-lowering?                                                                

  Searched for 2 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, reusable, and you should reuse them. They survive the lowering choice
  cleanly.

  Why they're portable. The typemaps and gcmaps are m3front-level synthesis, not
   M3CG artifacts. m3front computes them from semantic information it owns:
  per-type field offsets, which fields are traced refs, the inheritance chain,
  the method-selector ordering. The existing emission goes through M3CG init_*
  calls only because M3CG happens to be the data-emission channel — nothing in
  those maps is M3CG-shaped. They're constant data with a runtime-defined byte
  layout (what RTHeapMap.i3 and RTType consume). Under MSIR/LLVM the same
  m3front code computes the same bytes; MSIR emits them as init_*-equivalent
  constant ops; LLVM lowering emits them as @global = constant .... The runtime
  can't tell the difference.

  The single coupling that matters. The maps encode "traced field at byte offset
   X of this type." That's only valid if MSIR picks the same object layout
  m3front picks today — prefix-extension single inheritance, declaration-order
  fields with platform alignment, one-word typecell pointer in the header. If
  MSIR ever wanted to reorder fields (cache packing, escape-driven unboxing,
  separating traced from untraced into split allocations), the map generator
  would need to learn about it. As long as MSIR honors the existing layout —
  which the walkthrough did — the maps drop in unchanged.

  Consistency obligation between gcmap and addrspace(1). They serve different
  audiences (gcmap → runtime collector; addrspace(1) → LLVM optimizer) but have
  to agree: every addrspace(1) field must appear in the gcmap, every gcmap entry
   must correspond to an addrspace(1)-typed field in the LLVM struct. Since both
   are derived from the same m3front type structure, this falls out
  automatically — it's just an invariant the type-lowering pass needs to
  maintain.

  What gcmaps don't cover, and never will. They describe heap object internals.
  They don't describe stack roots — that's what conservative scanning handles
  today, and what statepoints would handle if/when we go stack-precise. The two
  are complementary, not competing: gcmaps stay as the heap-tracing source of
  truth; statepoint-generated maps (computed by LLVM post-regalloc) would only
  ever describe SSA-resident traced refs at safepoints. If we never adopt
  statepoints, gcmaps + conservative scan covers everything; if we do, gcmaps
  still cover heap, statepoints add stack precision on top.

  So the practical implication is reassuring: the RTTI/GC-metadata pipeline is 
  one of the cheapest pieces of the MSIR project. No new map generator, no
  runtime changes, no format negotiation. The existing m3front machinery just
  keeps running, and MSIR/LLVM consumes its output the same way M3CG does today.

✻ Churned for 1m 8s

❯ Is the next step to sketch out an MSIR design for the whole language or piece 
   by piece. Can we plan how to attack that problem?                            

⏺ Piece by piece — but with a thin spine sketched first so the pieces stay
  coherent. The OBJECT walkthrough already showed why: walking concrete features
   forces decisions that abstract spec-writing dodges, but those decisions only
  compose if the cross-cutting axes are picked early.

  What I'd recommend

  Don't write a full MSIR spec up front. Premature formalization encodes
  assumptions you haven't tested, and Modula‑3 has enough corners that a
  paper-only design will be wrong in places that only show up in lowering. The
  OBJECT walkthrough produced more useful design than the abstract type system
  sketch in §1.2 did, because it forced commitments (header-is-one-word,
  typecell-not-traced, prefix-extension layout, named selectors at MSIR level).

  Don't go feature-by-feature without a spine, either. EH, GC, dispatch, and the
   type system interact: deciding "TRY lowers to invoke" and "traced refs are
  addrspace(1)" independently can produce a landingpad that drops the
  address-space, which only surfaces at runtime. The cross-cutting axes need to
  be picked once, coherently.

  Concrete plan

  Step 1 — spine sketch (small, written down). A short document that just names
  the cross-cutting axes and pins each at low resolution:
  1. Type system kinds (scalar/aggregate/reference, with gc_ref vs gc_slot
  distinction).
  2. Procedure shape (typed-SSA, block params, flat CFG with structured EH
  envelopes only for TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY).
  3. GC contract (addrspace(1), gc.load/gc.store ops, m3front-emitted gcmaps
  reused unchanged, conservative stack scan as the MVP root model).
  4. EH contract (structured envelopes in IR, lowered to
  invoke/landingpad/resume for Has_stack_walker targets, setjmp scaffold
  otherwise).
  5. Module/init contract (globals, root registration, init-order via existing
  m3front machinery).
  6. Intrinsic surface (the M3-distinctive ops: dispatch, narrow, istype,
  typecase, range_check, nil_check, subscript_check, set_*, bitfield_*).

  This is maybe 3–5 pages, not a spec. Its job is to be the thing every
  walkthrough refers back to, and the thing that gets amended (not rewritten)
  when a walkthrough exposes a missing axis.

  Step 2 — four more vertical walkthroughs, in priority order:
  - TRY / EXCEPT / FINALLY — riskiest piece, exercises the EH envelope shape,
  exposes the Start_try/End_try ordering hazard's MSIR replacement.
  - Open arrays (ARRAY OF T parameter, multi-dim slicing, subscript checks) —
  most M3-distinctive feature after objects; pins the dope-vector ABI.
  - Module init + a global of object type — smallest, but the MVP can't run
  without it; pins root registration and init-order.
  - Nested procedure with uplevel access — forces the lambda-lift-vs-static-link
   decision; small but architectural.

  After those four, the union of decisions covers ≥80% of the design. Sets,
  subrange checks, VAR/READONLY parameters, WITH, bitfields — these slot into
  existing categories rather than opening new axes. They can be specified by
  analogy.

  Step 3 — write the actual spec as synthesis, only after step 2. By that point
  the spec is mostly cleanup of decisions already made; the doc becomes a
  reference rather than a design exercise.

  Step 4 — in parallel with step 2 or 3, start the m3-sys/msir package. Just the
   typed in-memory IR + textual printer. No m3front integration, no LLVM
  emitter. The job of this package is to be the thing the walkthroughs typecheck
   against — when we sketch MSIR text, it should round-trip through this
  package. That keeps the design grounded.

  Step 5 — only then, start the engineering work: m3front MSIR builder, LLVM
  emitter, first compilable program.

  Two artifacts worth maintaining as we go

  - MSIR.md keeps the strategic narrative (what it is, why bypass M3CG,
  migration phases). The current doc is fine; it'll grow.
  - MSIR-design.md (new) — the spine from step 1, plus a running list of decided
   axioms and open questions. Every walkthrough produces entries for both. Keeps
   cross-cutting decisions out of individual walkthrough docs.

  A decided entry looks like: "Object header is a single word holding a typecell
   pointer. Typecell pointer is not a traced ref. Forces: dispatch lowers to 
  plain load + indirect call; no gc.load on header reads."

  An open entry looks like: "Stack-precise GC via statepoints — out of MVP scope
   but addrspace(1) decision must not preclude it."

  This shape — spine-first, walkthrough-driven, spec-as-cleanup,
  design-doc-as-running-record — is roughly how Rust did MIR and Swift did SIL.
  Both projects tried to write the spec first and got it wrong; both ended up
  reverse-engineering the spec from the implementation. We can skip one of those
   iterations.

  What I'd do this week

  If you want to keep momentum: TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY walkthrough next, same shape
  as OBJECT — source M3 fragment, MSIR sketch, LLVM lowering, decisions
  surfaced. It's the highest-leverage of the remaining slices and it'll tell us
  whether the structured-EH-envelope idea actually works or whether we need to
  flatten earlier than I claimed.

  Want me to do that, or would you rather I start on the spine document first?

✻ Sautéed for 1m 28s

❯ Let's do that                                                                 

⏺ TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY walkthrough. Same structure as OBJECT — source, MSIR, LLVM,
   decisions surfaced.

  ---
  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;
  IMPORT Wr, Stdio, Text;

  EXCEPTION NotFound;
  EXCEPTION Bad(TEXT);                              (* exception with a value *)

  PROCEDURE Lookup(key: TEXT): INTEGER RAISES {NotFound, Bad} =
    BEGIN
      IF Text.Equal(key, "good") THEN RETURN 42 END;
      IF Text.Equal(key, "bad")  THEN RAISE Bad("malformed") END;
      RAISE NotFound;
    END Lookup;

  (* TRY / EXCEPT with two named clauses, one binding a value *)
  PROCEDURE WithLog(key: TEXT): INTEGER =
    VAR n: INTEGER;
    BEGIN
      TRY
        n := Lookup(key);
      EXCEPT
      | NotFound      => n := -1;
      | Bad(msg)      => Wr.PutText(Stdio.stderr, msg);  n := -2;
      END;
      RETURN n;
    END WithLog;

  (* TRY / FINALLY with RETURN crossing the envelope, and propagation *)
  PROCEDURE WithCleanup(key: TEXT): INTEGER RAISES {NotFound, Bad} =
    VAR n: INTEGER;
    BEGIN
      Wr.PutText(Stdio.stderr, "open\n");
      TRY
        n := Lookup(key);
        RETURN n;                    (* must run FINALLY before returning *)
      FINALLY
        Wr.PutText(Stdio.stderr, "close\n");   (* runs on normal AND exceptional
   exit *)
      END;
    END WithCleanup;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: named exceptions (no value), exceptions with a value,
  multi-clause EXCEPT with value-binding, propagation, FINALLY on
  normal-completion path, FINALLY on exception path, RETURN-through-FINALLY (the
   nontrivial case).

  ---
  2. MSIR

  EH is a structured envelope around CFG fragments. Inside the envelope, the CFG
   is flat (basic blocks with parameters). The envelope carries the
  handler/finally structure that the lowering pass needs.

  module Demo

    ; ---- exception declarations ---------------------------------------------
    ; Each one emits an immutable identity global. The "value type" is part of
    ; the declaration; handlers either bind it or ignore it.

    exception @NotFound : void
    exception @Bad      : gc_ref Text

    ; ---- procedures ---------------------------------------------------------

    proc @Lookup(key: gc_ref Text) -> i64
         raises { @NotFound, @Bad }
    entry:
      %is_good = call @Text.Equal(key, @str.good) -> i1
      cond_br %is_good, good, b1
    good:
      ret 42
    b1:
      %is_bad = call @Text.Equal(key, @str.bad) -> i1
      cond_br %is_bad, bad, nf
    bad:
      raise @Bad, @str.malformed
    nf:
      raise @NotFound

    ; ---- TRY / EXCEPT --------------------------------------------------------

    proc @WithLog(key: gc_ref Text) -> i64
    entry:
      try {
      body:
        %v = invoke @Lookup(key) -> i64        ; "invoke" form: may raise into
  envelope
        br merge(%v)
      } except {
        | @NotFound          => block h1:
                                  br merge(-1)
        | @Bad(msg)          => block h2(msg: gc_ref Text):
                                  call @Wr.PutText(@Stdio.stderr, msg)
                                  br merge(-2)
      }
    merge(%n: i64):
      ret %n

    ; ---- TRY / FINALLY (with RETURN through cleanup) -------------------------

    proc @WithCleanup(key: gc_ref Text) -> i64
         raises { @NotFound, @Bad }
    entry:
      call @Wr.PutText(@Stdio.stderr, @str.open)
      try_finally {
      body:
        %n = invoke @Lookup(key) -> i64        ; may raise; FINALLY still runs
        ret_through_envelope %n                 ; non-local exit: FINALLY then
  return
      } finally {
        call @Wr.PutText(@Stdio.stderr, @str.close)
      }
      ; falling out of try_finally body without ret_through_envelope reaches
  here
      unreachable

  Three things worth noticing about the IR shape:

  - invoke is an MSIR-level distinction, not just an LLVM thing. A call inside a
   try/try_finally body that might raise an exception covered by the envelope
  uses invoke; everything else is plain call. The decision is local to the
  envelope and computable from the callee's raises {…} set.
  - Handler blocks are first-class blocks with parameters. Bad(msg) becomes
  block h2(msg: gc_ref Text). The handler dispatch (which exception ID matched?)
   is implicit in the envelope's clause list — not spelled out as a switch in
  MSIR. Lowering generates the switch.
  - ret_through_envelope is the only special non-local exit op. Plain ret inside
   an envelope body is a static error in MSIR — every cross-envelope exit must
  be explicit so the lowering pass can thread the cleanup. This is the MSIR
  replacement for the M3CG Start_try/End_try ordering hazard: the structure is
  in the IR, the ordering is mechanical.

  ---
  3. LLVM IR

  Lowering uses the existing _M3Exc/Itanium machinery (the ex_stack model).
  M3-exception identity is carried inside the _M3Exc activation record; the
  C++-level catch is always for _M3Exc, with M3-level dispatch on act->exception
   after the landing pad. This matches what the runtime already does — no new
  C++ types per M3 exception.

  Boilerplate:

  %RTExFrame = type { ptr, ptr }                  ; { exception_id, value }
  declare ptr @_M3Exc_typeinfo()                  ; existing in the runtime

  ; Each EXCEPTION declaration becomes a constant identity global.
  @NotFound = constant i8 0
  @Bad      = constant i8 0

  declare void @RTStack__ThrowM3Exc(ptr) noreturn
  declare ptr @__cxa_begin_catch(ptr)
  declare void @__cxa_end_catch()
  declare i32 @llvm.eh.typeid.for(ptr)

  Note: the i8 0 body of the exception globals is irrelevant — it's the address
  that's the identity.

  WithLog — TRY / EXCEPT lowering

  define i64 @WithLog(ptr addrspace(1) %key) personality ptr
  @__m3_personality_v0 {
  entry:
    ; ----- body of TRY: invoke instead of call
    %v = invoke i64 @Lookup(ptr addrspace(1) %key)
           to label %merge_pre unwind label %lpad

  merge_pre:
    br label %merge
    ; (this trampoline goes away after SimplifyCFG; shown for clarity)

  ; ----- single C++-level landingpad catches _M3Exc
  lpad:
    %lp = landingpad { ptr, i32 }
            catch ptr @_M3Exc_typeinfo
    %exc.ptr = extractvalue { ptr, i32 } %lp, 0
    %act     = call ptr @__cxa_begin_catch(ptr %exc.ptr)   ; %act = ptr to 
  %RTExFrame
    %id.p    = getelementptr %RTExFrame, ptr %act, i32 0, i32 0
    %id      = load ptr, ptr %id.p

    ; ----- M3-level dispatch on exception identity
    switch ptr %id, label %unhandled [
      ptr @NotFound, label %h1_pre
      ptr @Bad,      label %h2_pre
    ]

  h1_pre:
    call void @__cxa_end_catch()
    br label %h1
  h1:
    br label %merge_n1
  merge_n1:
    br label %merge_with(-1)

  h2_pre:
    ; bind value: act->value
    %v.p = getelementptr %RTExFrame, ptr %act, i32 0, i32 1
    %msg = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr %v.p
    call void @__cxa_end_catch()
    br label %h2
  h2:
    call void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1) @Stdio.stderr, ptr addrspace(1)
  %msg)
    br label %merge_with(-2)

  unhandled:
    ; nothing in WithLog's raises set; resume propagates
    call void @__cxa_end_catch()
    resume { ptr, i32 } %lp

  merge:
    %n = phi i64 [ %v, %merge_pre ], [ -1, %merge_n1 ], [ -2, %h2 ]
    ret i64 %n
  }

  WithCleanup — TRY / FINALLY with RETURN-through-cleanup

  The FINALLY block is materialized twice at LLVM level: once on the
  normal/return path, once on the exception/cleanup path. This is the standard
  duplication strategy and keeps each path SSA-clean.

  define i64 @WithCleanup(ptr addrspace(1) %key) personality ptr
  @__m3_personality_v0 {
  entry:
    call void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1) @Stdio.stderr, ptr addrspace(1)
  @str.open)
    %n = invoke i64 @Lookup(ptr addrspace(1) %key)
           to label %ret_finally unwind label %cleanup_lpad

  ; ----- normal-completion path: FINALLY then return
  ret_finally:
    call void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1) @Stdio.stderr, ptr addrspace(1)
  @str.close)
    ret i64 %n

  ; ----- exception path: cleanup landingpad runs FINALLY then resumes
  cleanup_lpad:
    %lp = landingpad { ptr, i32 } cleanup
    call void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1) @Stdio.stderr, ptr addrspace(1)
  @str.close)
    resume { ptr, i32 } %lp
  }

  Annotations on what survived and what was forged:

  - A single C++ catch type (_M3Exc), M3-level dispatch via switch on the loaded
   exception identity. This matches the existing ex_stack runtime; no
  per-M3-exception C++ types.
  - The landingpad clause shape is target-conditional. Has_stack_walker targets
  emit landingpad ... catch ptr @_M3Exc_typeinfo; non-stack-walker targets would
   emit a different shape (or fall through to a setjmp/longjmp scaffold). MSIR's
   try envelope is identical in both cases — the lowering pass picks.
  - FINALLY duplication: normal-completion path (ret_finally) and exception path
   (cleanup_lpad) both contain the FINALLY body. For larger FINALLY blocks the
  lowering can choose to outline to a helper function called from both sites;
  for small bodies, duplicate. LLVM's late passes will sink/merge as
  appropriate.
  - ret_through_envelope lowered cleanly: the lowering walks outward from the
  ret_through_envelope site, threading every enclosing FINALLY in order before
  issuing the ret. There's no separate "phase 2 unwind" machinery — it's normal
  control flow.
  - __m3_personality_v0 can be a thin wrapper over __gxx_personality_v0 since
  the actual catch logic is M3-level, not C++-level. Or just use
  __gxx_personality_v0 directly.

  ---
  4. Decisions this walkthrough forces

  1. EH envelope is structured at MSIR level; CFG is flat inside. Specifically:
  only try { … } except { … }, try_finally { … } finally { … }, and raise are
  envelope-shaped. Everything else is basic-block CFG. This is the minimum
  structure needed to avoid the M3CG ordering hazard.
  2. Plain ret inside an envelope body is a static error. Cross-envelope exits
  use ret_through_envelope (or br_through_envelope for non-local jumps from EXIT
   in a LOOP). Forces the IR to make non-local control visible.
  3. invoke is computed locally, from the envelope shape and the callee's raises
   {…} set. A call to a non-raising procedure inside a TRY stays as call, not
  invoke — that distinction matters for codegen quality.
  4. M3 exception identity is a global address, not a C++ type. Each EXCEPTION
  declaration emits one identity global; the activation record points to it.
  C++-level dispatch sees only _M3Exc; M3-level dispatch is a switch. This is
  what the existing runtime expects, and it scales: 10,000 declared exceptions
  cost 10,000 globals, not 10,000 C++ RTTI entries.
  5. Handler value bindings are block parameters. Bad(msg) lowers to a load of
  the activation's value slot followed by branch into a parameterized block.
  Idiomatic SSA; no special "handler local" concept.
  6. FINALLY is duplicated, not shared. Normal path and cleanup path each carry
  the FINALLY body. For large FINALLY bodies the duplication can be outlined;
  for small, leave it. SimplifyCFG / Inliner handle the optimization.
  7. raises {…} on procedures is metadata, not just documentation. It's how the
  lowering pass decides whether a call needs invoke. m3front already computes
  this; MSIR carries it forward as a first-class procedure attribute.
  8. __m3_personality_v0 is a real-but-trivial function that delegates to the
  existing C++ personality. Centralizing the personality function makes future
  evolution (e.g. a setjmp-based personality on ex_frame targets) a one-line
  lowering change.

  ---
  5. Two corners we ducked

  - RAISE inside FINALLY (a "cleanup raises while another exception is in
  flight"). C++ semantics terminate; M3's semantics are: the new exception
  replaces the in-flight one. Lowering: the FINALLY's own landing pad calls
  __cxa_end_catch for the prior exception before raising the new one. Worth a
  paragraph in the spine doc, not a walkthrough.
  - LOCK statement. LOCK m DO body END is sugar for m.acquire(); TRY body 
  FINALLY m.release() END. Falls out of the FINALLY lowering for free, no new
  MSIR construct needed.

  ---
  What this exposed about the spine

  Two cross-cutting axes need pinning before the next walkthrough:

  - Procedure attribute set, including raises {…}. The OBJECT walkthrough mostly
   ignored procedure attributes; this one needs them. Probably also noreturn
  (for RAISE-only paths), cc (calling convention), and eventually gc "modula3"
  once we use an LLVM GC strategy.
  - Non-local control vocabulary. ret_through_envelope is one; EXIT from LOOP
  through enclosing TRY-FINALLY is the same shape; EXITS from nested LOOPs
  likewise. Probably one op family — call it unwind_to label, args — that
  handles all of them.

  I'd add both to the running design doc before the open-array walkthrough. Want
   me to sketch that doc next, or push on with open arrays?

⏺ MSIR-design.md written at repo root — 12 axes/decisions/open-questions
