  ---
  Open-array walkthrough

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  (* 1D open array parameter *)
  PROCEDURE Sum(a: ARRAY OF INTEGER): INTEGER =
    VAR s: INTEGER := 0;
    BEGIN
      FOR i := 0 TO NUMBER(a) - 1 DO
        s := s + a[i];
      END;
      RETURN s;
    END Sum;

  (* 2D open array parameter *)
  PROCEDURE Sum2D(m: ARRAY OF ARRAY OF INTEGER): INTEGER =
    VAR s: INTEGER := 0;
    BEGIN
      FOR i := 0 TO LAST(m, 0) DO
        FOR j := 0 TO LAST(m, 1) DO
          s := s + m[i, j];
        END;
      END;
      RETURN s;
    END Sum2D;

  (* Heap-allocated REF ARRAY OF *)
  PROCEDURE Make(n: INTEGER): REF ARRAY OF INTEGER =
    VAR r := NEW(REF ARRAY OF INTEGER, n);
    BEGIN
      FOR i := 0 TO n - 1 DO r[i] := i * i END;
      RETURN r;
    END Make;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: 1D and 2D open-array parameter ABI; NUMBER/LAST shape
  queries; subscript bounds checks; multi-dim address arithmetic; heap
  allocation of REF ARRAY OF T (dope-prefix layout); deref-to-fat-pointer (r[i]
  accessing through the heap object).

  2. MSIR

  module Demo

    ; ---- types --------------------------------------------------------------
    ; Open arrays are STRUCTURAL types of fixed shape: { data, sizes... }.
    ; Rank is part of the type.
    type openarray<1> i64 = { untraced ptr i64, i64 }
    type openarray<2> i64 = { untraced ptr i64, i64, i64 }

    ; REF ARRAY OF INTEGER is a heap-allocated dope-prefix layout: the
    ; size word(s) are followed by the data inline.
    type @RArrInt = object_layout {
      header_typecell: ptr_const                  ; standard header
      size0: i64
      data:  flex i64                             ; trailing flex array
    }

    ; ---- ops introduced ------------------------------------------------------
    ;   openarray.size      oa, k                  -> i64
    ;   openarray.data      oa                     -> untraced ptr T
    ;   openarray.elem_addr oa, idx0[, idx1...]    -> untraced ptr T   ;
  arithmetic only
    ;   openarray.deref     gc_ref @RArr<T>        -> openarray<rank> T
    ;   openarray.new       <T, rank>, sz0[, sz1...]  -> gc_ref @RArr<T>
    ;   subscript_check     idx, bound             ; raises on fail
    ;
    ; openarray.elem_addr does pure address arithmetic; bounds checks are
    ; SEPARATE subscript_check ops so they can be eliminated independently.

    proc @Sum(a: openarray<1> i64) -> i64
    entry:
      %n = openarray.size a, 0
      br loop(0, 0)
    loop(%i: i64, %s: i64):
      %lt = icmp slt %i, %n
      cond_br %lt, body, exit
    body:
      subscript_check %i, %n
      %ep = openarray.elem_addr a, %i
      %v  = load i64, %ep
      %s2 = iadd %s, %v
      %i2 = iadd %i, 1
      br loop(%i2, %s2)
    exit:
      ret %s

    proc @Sum2D(m: openarray<2> i64) -> i64
    entry:
      %n0 = openarray.size m, 0
      %n1 = openarray.size m, 1
      br outer(0, 0)
    outer(%i: i64, %s: i64):
      %lt0 = icmp slt %i, %n0
      cond_br %lt0, inner_init, exit
    inner_init:
      br inner(0, %i, %s)
    inner(%j: i64, %i: i64, %s: i64):
      %lt1 = icmp slt %j, %n1
      cond_br %lt1, ibody, ostep(%i, %s)
    ibody:
      subscript_check %i, %n0
      subscript_check %j, %n1
      %ep = openarray.elem_addr m, %i, %j         ; lowers to i*n1 + j
      %v  = load i64, %ep
      %s2 = iadd %s, %v
      %j2 = iadd %j, 1
      br inner(%j2, %i, %s2)
    ostep(%i: i64, %s: i64):
      %i2 = iadd %i, 1
      br outer(%i2, %s)
    exit:
      ret %s

    proc @Make(n: i64) -> gc_ref @RArrInt
    entry:
      %r = openarray.new <element=i64, rank=1>, n     ; runtime alloc + dope set
      %a = openarray.deref %r                         ; fat pointer view
      br loop(0)
    loop(%i: i64):
      %lt = icmp slt %i, n
      cond_br %lt, body, ret_block
    body:
      %sq    = imul %i, %i
      %sz    = openarray.size %a, 0
      subscript_check %i, %sz
      %ep    = openarray.elem_addr %a, %i           ; data ptr is in
  addrspace(1)
      store i64 %sq, %ep
      %i2    = iadd %i, 1
      br loop(%i2)
    ret_block:
      ret %r

  Three things worth noticing:

  - openarray.elem_addr is pure arithmetic; subscript_check is separate. This
  factoring is essential: it lets LLVM's range analysis remove the check when
  the index is provably in range (e.g. derived from the FOR loop bound 0 TO 
  NUMBER(a)-1), without removing the address computation. Bundling them would
  require a custom pass.
  - openarray.deref constructs a fat-pointer view from a heap REF object. The
  data pointer in the resulting fat pointer inherits the address space of the
  source — addrspace(1) for Make's heap allocation, plain addrspace(0) for a
  stack-allocated SUBARRAY (a future walkthrough).
  - The 2D subscript checks (subscript_check %i, %n0 and subscript_check %j, 
  %n1) are mostly redundant given the FOR loop bounds. They stay in the IR as
  explicit ops; LLVM's IndVar+ScalarEvolution removes them at -O1+. The
  MSIR-level optimization "elide check when index dominates a known range" is a
  peephole on top, not a prerequisite.

  3. LLVM IR

  I'll show Sum and Make in full; for Sum2D the pattern is the same with one
  extra layer of arithmetic — I'll just sketch the elem_addr lowering.

  declare void @RTException__Raise(ptr) noreturn
  declare ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__NewArray(ptr, i64)

  @SubscriptOutOfRange = external constant i8
  @RArrInt_ti          = external constant i8

  ; --- Sum: 1D open-array parameter -------------------------------------------
  ; openarray<1> i64 lowers to { ptr, i64 } passed by value (LLVM ABI may split)

  define i64 @Sum({ ptr, i64 } %a) {
  entry:
    %data = extractvalue { ptr, i64 } %a, 0
    %n    = extractvalue { ptr, i64 } %a, 1
    br label %loop
  loop:
    %i = phi i64 [ 0, %entry ], [ %i.next, %body ]
    %s = phi i64 [ 0, %entry ], [ %s.next, %body ]
    %lt = icmp slt i64 %i, %n
    br i1 %lt, label %check, label %exit
  check:
    ; subscript_check %i, %n
    %inrange = icmp ult i64 %i, %n
    br i1 %inrange, label %body, label %fail
  body:
    ; openarray.elem_addr — plain GEP
    %ep    = getelementptr i64, ptr %data, i64 %i
    %v     = load i64, ptr %ep
    %s.next = add i64 %s, %v
    %i.next = add i64 %i, 1
    br label %loop
  fail:
    call void @RTException__Raise(ptr @SubscriptOutOfRange)
    unreachable
  exit:
    ret i64 %s
  }

  After -O1 (IndVarSimplify + range analysis): the check block disappears, loop
  falls straight into body. That's the win.

  Sum2D's openarray.elem_addr m, %i, %j lowers as:

    ; m : { ptr, i64, i64 }   (data, n0, n1)
    ; address = data + (i * n1 + j) * sizeof(i64)
    %row.off = mul i64 %i, %n1
    %off     = add i64 %row.off, %j
    %ep      = getelementptr i64, ptr %data, i64 %off

  Row-major. The dope's n1 is the inner stride. Multi-dim generalizes by
  recursive accumulation.

  Make lowers to:

  ; @RArrInt layout: { ptr typecell, i64 size0, [0 x i64] data }
  %RArrInt = type { ptr, i64, [0 x i64] }

  define ptr addrspace(1) @Make(i64 %n) {
  entry:
    ; openarray.new — allocator stamps typecell + size; returns gc_ref
    %r = call ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__NewArray(ptr @RArrInt_ti, i64 %n)
    ; openarray.deref — extract { data, size } fat pointer view
    %size.p = getelementptr %RArrInt, ptr addrspace(1) %r, i32 0, i32 1
    %size   = load i64, ptr addrspace(1) %size.p, !invariant.load !0
    %data.p = getelementptr %RArrInt, ptr addrspace(1) %r, i32 0, i32 2, i64 0
    ; data.p is in addrspace(1) — it points into the GC heap
    br label %loop
  loop:
    %i = phi i64 [ 0, %entry ], [ %i.next, %body ]
    %lt = icmp slt i64 %i, %n
    br i1 %lt, label %check, label %ret_b
  check:
    %inrange = icmp ult i64 %i, %size
    br i1 %inrange, label %body, label %fail
  body:
    %sq    = mul i64 %i, %i
    %ep    = getelementptr i64, ptr addrspace(1) %data.p, i64 %i
    store i64 %sq, ptr addrspace(1) %ep
    %i.next = add i64 %i, 1
    br label %loop
  fail:
    call void @RTException__Raise(ptr @SubscriptOutOfRange)
    unreachable
  ret_b:
    ret ptr addrspace(1) %r
  }

  !0 = !{}     ; size of a REF ARRAY OF is set at allocation and never changes

  Annotations:

  - !invariant.load on the size load. Once allocated, a REF ARRAY OF does not
  change size. LLVM can hoist the size load out of inner loops aggressively.
  (M3CG-via-clang has no way to communicate this.)
  - %data.p is ptr addrspace(1) because it points inside a GC-heap object. The
  store and load go through the same addrspace.
  - Liveness of %r across the loop is the latent hazard recorded as O12 in
  MSIR-design.md: under register-allocator pressure, LLVM might drop %r and keep
   only %data.p. The conservative stack scanner finds %data.p (it's a
  pointer-shaped value), but %data.p points into the interior of a heap object —
   the conservative scanner wants object-base pointers. Bartlett collectors
  handle interior pointers by treating any pointer that lands in a heap page as
  pinning the containing object, so this is probably safe under the existing
  collector, but it's the kind of correctness property that needs to be tested
  under heavy GC stress, not assumed.

  4. Decisions this walkthrough forces

  1. Open arrays are structural fat-pointer types with rank in the type. Lowered
   to LLVM { ptr, i64, ... } structs; ABI splitting is LLVM's job.
  2. Dope-prefix layout for REF ARRAY OF T: typecell header, then size word(s),
  then inline data via flex array. Single allocation; matches existing runtime
  expectations.
  3. openarray.elem_addr and subscript_check are separate ops. Address
  arithmetic and bounds check decoupled so range analysis can eliminate one
  without the other.
  4. Multi-dim is row-major with explicit per-dim sizes in the dope.
  openarray<2> T is { ptr, n0, n1 }, not { ptr, [n0 x [n1 x T]] }. The element
  address is i*n1 + j. Higher ranks generalize.
  5. openarray.deref carries the address space through. Heap-derived opens have
  data in addrspace(1); stack-derived (future SUBARRAY walkthrough) have data in
   addrspace(0). The op is polymorphic; the LLVM type carries the discriminator.
  6. The size load of REF ARRAY OF is !invariant.load. Like the typecell
  pointer, the size is set at allocation and never changes.
  7. Subscript checks are intrinsic ops, not runtime calls. Lowered to icmp ult
  + cold-call branch. LLVM optimizes them; M3CG-via-C can't communicate the
  structure.

  5. Updates to the spine doc

  The walkthrough resolves O3 (open-array dope ABI is now pinned) and tightens
  O12 (the interior-pointer / liveness hazard is now concrete and testable). Two
   new entries to add:

  - D13 (decided): open arrays are structural fat-pointer types { data, sizes...
   }; REF ARRAY OF T uses dope-prefix heap layout; address arithmetic and bounds
   checks are separate ops.
  - O13 (open): SUBARRAY semantics. SUBARRAY(a, lo, count) constructs a new fat
  pointer pointing into existing storage. Trivial for addrspace(0) opens; raises
   the interior-pointer-into-heap question for addrspace(1) opens. Defer to a
  future walkthrough.

  I'll roll D13/O13 into the doc.

  Where we are

  Three walkthroughs done (OBJECT, TRY, opens). Spine doc tracks 13 decisions
  and 13 open questions. The next walkthroughs that would close the largest
  remaining gaps:

  - Module init + a global of object type — closes O6 (init order) and exercises
   the gc_slot global pattern end-to-end. Smallest of the remaining; needed for
  the MVP because nothing runs without it.
  - Nested procedure with uplevel access — closes O4 (lambda-lift vs static
  link). Architectural; not large.
  - SUBARRAY — closes O13 just opened, and probably forces a refinement of O12
  (GC liveness). The most subtle remaining.
  - Procedure call with VAR/READONLY parameters — closes O10. Mostly mechanical;
   resolves how reference parameters interact with addrspace(1).

  I'd suggest module init next: it's the smallest, it's MVP-critical, and the
  SUBARRAY/liveness question deserves to be tackled with fresh eyes in its own
  session.

※ recap: Designing MSIR, a new Modula-3 semantic IR that bypasses M3CG to retarget CM3 to LLVM. Three walkthroughs done (OBJECT, TRY/EXCEPT, open arrays) and spine doc written; next walkthrough is module init. (disable recaps in /config)

⏺ Module init walkthrough.

  1. Source

  INTERFACE Demo;

  EXCEPTION Bad;

  TYPE
    T = OBJECT
      n: INTEGER := 0;
    METHODS
      inc() := Inc;
    END;

  VAR singleton: T;

  PROCEDURE Inc(self: T);

  END Demo.

  MODULE Demo;
  IMPORT Wr, Stdio;

  EXCEPTION Bad;

  TYPE
    T = OBJECT
      n: INTEGER := 0;
    METHODS
      inc() := Inc;
    END;

  VAR singleton: T;

  PROCEDURE Inc(self: T) =
    BEGIN
      INC(self.n);
    END Inc;

  BEGIN
    singleton := NEW(T);
    Wr.PutText(Stdio.stderr, "Demo initialized\n");
  END Demo.

  What this exercises: an EXCEPTION declaration, an OBJECT type with a
  default-initialized field, a module-level VAR of object type (traced global),
  a module body (BEGIN…END) that allocates and writes the global and calls into
  another module, and the cross-module dependency on Wr and Stdio that
  establishes init order.

  2. MSIR

  Module init has three sub-pieces that need IR-level representation: the init 
  procedure (the BEGIN…END body), the module descriptor (declarative record
  consumed by RTLinker), and registration (the side-channel by which the linker
  learns this module exists). MSIR makes all three first-class.

  module Demo
    imports {Wr, Stdio}
    exports {Demo}

    ; ---- exception identity --------------------------------------------------
    exception @Bad : void

    ; ---- type declaration ---------------------------------------------------
    type T = object {
      super:    <root>
      fields:   { n: i64 = 0 }                       ; default initializer
      methods:  [ inc: proc(gc_ref T) -> void ]
      defaults: [ inc := @Inc ]
      descriptor: @T_ti
    }

    ; ---- traced module global -----------------------------------------------
    global @Demo.singleton : gc_slot T = nil
    ; The "gc_slot" annotation tells the lowering pass:
    ;   - emit a write barrier on every store
    ;   - register the address as a GC root in the module descriptor
    ;   - emit nil-initialized data (not BSS — a typed null)

    ; ---- procedures ---------------------------------------------------------
    proc @Inc(self: gc_ref T) -> void
    entry:
      %p  = field_addr self, T.n
      %v  = load i64, %p
      %v2 = iadd %v, 1
      store i64 %v2, %p
      ret

    ; ---- module init body ---------------------------------------------------
    ; The MODULE's BEGIN ... END becomes a procedure with no caller-visible
    ; signature. RTLinker is the only caller; idempotency is RTLinker's job.
    proc @Demo__init() -> void
    entry:
      %obj = new T                                  ; runtime alloc + header
      ; field defaults are applied by 'new' from the typecell's default vector;
      ; T.n := 0 is in there. No explicit field_store needed.
      gc.store @Demo.singleton, %obj
      call @Wr.PutText(@Stdio.stderr, @str.demo_init)
      ret

    ; ---- module descriptor --------------------------------------------------
    ; Synthesized; not source-visible. Lowered to a constant struct plus an
    ; @llvm.global_ctors entry that registers it with RTLinker at process
    ; startup. RTLinker topologically sorts by imports, then calls each
    ; module's init proc exactly once.
    module_descriptor @Demo {
      name              : "Demo"
      init              : @Demo__init
      imports           : [@Wr, @Stdio]
      type_descriptors  : [@T_ti]
      exception_globals : [@Bad]
      traced_globals    : [{ addr: @Demo.singleton, type: @T_ti }]
    }

  Three things worth noticing:

  - The module descriptor is the only declarative entity in MSIR. Everything
  else is procedural (procs, loads, stores). The descriptor is what makes init
  order, root registration, and type registration handleable as data rather than
   code. Lowering emits four things from it: a constant struct, an
  @llvm.global_ctors constructor that calls RTLinker__AddUnit, the externalized
  symbol declarations for cross-module imports, and the registration of
  traced_globals as GC roots.
  - @Demo__init has no idempotency guard in the MSIR. RTLinker provides "called
  exactly once, after all imports are init'd, in dependency order" as a
  precondition. Putting the guard inside the proc would duplicate work the
  runtime already does.
  - Field defaults are expressed at the type, not at the init site. T.n := 0 is
  part of the type's default vector; new T reads it. This matches the existing
  RTType machinery — RTAllocator__New zero-fills and applies defaults from the
  typecell. No field_store needed in the init body for a default-zero field.

  3. LLVM IR

  ; ---- exception identity ---------------------------------------------------
  @Bad = constant i8 0                           ; address-only; the byte is 
  irrelevant

  ; ---- type descriptor (RTType-shaped) --------------------------------------
  %RTType = type { ptr, i32, ptr, i32, [1 x ptr] }
  @T_tracemap = external constant i8             ; emitted by GC layout pass
  @T_ti = constant %RTType {
    ptr null,                                    ; super
    i32 17,                                      ; typecode
    ptr @T_tracemap,
    i32 1,
    [1 x ptr] [ ptr @Inc ]
  }

  ; ---- traced module global -------------------------------------------------
  @Demo.singleton = global ptr addrspace(1) null

  ; ---- Inc ------------------------------------------------------------------
  %T_layout = type { ptr, i64 }                  ; { typecell, n }

  define void @Inc(ptr addrspace(1) %self) {
    %p  = getelementptr %T_layout, ptr addrspace(1) %self, i32 0, i32 1
    %v  = load i64, ptr addrspace(1) %p
    %v2 = add i64 %v, 1
    store i64 %v2, ptr addrspace(1) %p
    ret void
  }

  ; ---- imports from other modules -------------------------------------------
  @Stdio.stderr = external global ptr addrspace(1)
  declare void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1), ptr addrspace(1))

  ; ---- string literal -------------------------------------------------------
  @str.demo_init = constant [18 x i8] c"Demo initialized\0A\00"

  ; ---- runtime entry points -------------------------------------------------
  declare ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr)
  declare void            @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr, ptr addrspace(1))
  declare void            @RTLinker__AddUnit(ptr)

  ; ---- init body ------------------------------------------------------------
  define void @Demo__init() {
  entry:
    %obj = call ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr @T_ti)

    ; gc.store @Demo.singleton, %obj
    call void @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr @Demo.singleton, ptr addrspace(1) %obj)
    store ptr addrspace(1) %obj, ptr @Demo.singleton

    ; cross-module call
    %s   = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr @Stdio.stderr
    ; @str.demo_init is plain rodata (addrspace 0); the M3 TEXT type carries
    ; literal-text discriminant in its header. Bitcast / wrap as TEXT happens
    ; at MSIR level via a 'literal_text' op (omitted here for brevity).
    call void @Wr__PutText(ptr addrspace(1) %s,
                            ptr addrspace(1) bitcast (ptr @str.demo_init to ptr
  addrspace(1)))
    ret void
  }

  ; ---- module descriptor ----------------------------------------------------
  ; Layout MUST match what RTLinker reads. Reusing the existing schema is the
  ; whole point — runtime does not need to learn a new format.

  %ModuleDesc = type {
    ptr,                                ; name (TEXT ptr or C-string ptr)
    ptr,                                ; init proc
    i32, [0 x ptr],                     ; n_imports,    imports[]
    i32, [0 x ptr],                     ; n_types,      type_descriptors[]
    i32, [0 x ptr],                     ; n_exceptions, exception_globals[]
    i32, [0 x { ptr, ptr }]             ; n_globals,    [(addr, typeinfo)]
  }

  @Demo.name        = constant [5 x i8] c"Demo\00"
  @Wr.descriptor    = external constant ptr             ; opaque to us
  @Stdio.descriptor = external constant ptr

  @Demo.descriptor = constant {
    ptr, ptr,
    i32, [2 x ptr],
    i32, [1 x ptr],
    i32, [1 x ptr],
    i32, [1 x { ptr, ptr }]
  } {
    ptr @Demo.name,
    ptr @Demo__init,
    i32 2,
      [2 x ptr] [ ptr @Wr.descriptor, ptr @Stdio.descriptor ],
    i32 1,
      [1 x ptr] [ ptr @T_ti ],
    i32 1,
      [1 x ptr] [ ptr @Bad ],
    i32 1,
      [1 x { ptr, ptr }] [ { ptr, ptr } { ptr @Demo.singleton, ptr @T_ti } ]
  }

  ; ---- registration constructor (runs at process startup) -------------------
  define internal void @Demo.register() {
    call void @RTLinker__AddUnit(ptr @Demo.descriptor)
    ret void
  }

  @llvm.global_ctors = appending global [1 x { i32, ptr, ptr }] [
    { i32, ptr, ptr } { i32 65535, ptr @Demo.register, ptr null }
  ]

  ; ---- root registration appendix -------------------------------------------
  ; Listing @Demo.singleton in the module descriptor (above) is sufficient
  ; for RTLinker to register it as a root during init walk. No separate
  ; @llvm.used or section trickery is required — the runtime already walks
  ; the descriptor's traced_globals list.

  Annotations on what survived and what was forged:

  - @llvm.global_ctors is the standard LLVM hook for "run this before main."
  Each module emits exactly one constructor, registering itself. RTLinker
  collects all registrations and then runs the inits in the right order. This
  separation matters: constructors run in arbitrary order, init must run in
  dependency order, so the constructor cannot be the init.
  - The module descriptor schema is the runtime's contract, not MSIR's
  invention. Whatever shape RTLinker reads today must be what MSIR emits. The
  schema above is illustrative; the real lowering reads the existing
  RTLinker.Module definition and matches it byte-for-byte.
  - Cross-module symbols are plain external declarations. Mangling
  (Module__Proc) matches what the C backend emits, so the platform linker
  resolves cross-module references the same way regardless of which backend
  produced which translation unit. This is what lets MSIR-compiled and
  C-compiled modules link together during incremental migration.
  - @T_tracemap is external constant because it's emitted by a separate part of
  the lowering (the GC layout pass). Reusing m3front's gcmap synthesis (D6)
  means MSIR doesn't compute it; it just declares it and lets the linker find
  it.
  - No idempotency guard inside @Demo__init. RTLinker's contract is "exactly
  once after imports." Adding a guard would be wasted code.
  - Field default n := 0 doesn't appear in @Demo__init — it's applied by
  RTAllocator__New from the typecell. Larger non-zero defaults would be in the
  typecell's default vector; new copies them.

  4. Decisions this walkthrough forces

  1. Module descriptors are first-class MSIR entities, not synthesized at 
  lowering. They appear in the IR alongside procedures and types. This makes
  cross-module dependencies, root sets, and type registration auditable from
  MSIR text without running the lowering.
  2. One constructor per module, registers-only; init runs later under 
  RTLinker's control. Dependency order is solved by the runtime, not by emission
   order or LLVM.
  3. Init procs have no explicit idempotency or guard. RTLinker's "exactly once
  after imports" contract is the spec.
  4. Traced globals list goes in the module descriptor. No @llvm.used tricks, no
   special sections — the runtime already walks the descriptor.
  5. Module descriptor schema is runtime-defined, not MSIR-defined. MSIR's
  module_descriptor block is a structured view; the lowering emits the bytes
  RTLinker expects. Changing the schema requires runtime cooperation; MSIR
  cannot evolve it unilaterally.
  6. Cross-module name mangling matches the C backend. Module__Proc for
  procedures, Module.var (or equivalent) for globals. This is what makes hybrid
  MSIR+C builds link.
  7. Field defaults live in the typecell, not in init. RTAllocator__New applies
  defaults; no per-instance code is generated for default-initialized fields.

  5. Updates to the spine doc

  - O6 (module init order) → resolved by D14.
  - D14 (new): module descriptors are first-class MSIR entities; one constructor
   per module; RTLinker provides ordered idempotent init; descriptor schema
  matches existing runtime.
  - O14 (new): behavior when module init raises an unhandled exception. Likely
  caught by RTLinker which terminates the process; needs to match existing
  semantics. Defer to runtime audit.
  - O15 (new): generic instantiations. Each instantiation produces a distinct
  module descriptor; how does init order treat them? Probably as ordinary
  modules with synthesized names, but worth confirming. Defer to a generics
  walkthrough.

⏺ ---
  Where we are now

  Four walkthroughs done. The spine doc tracks 14 decisions and 14 open
  questions; the remaining MVP-blocking pieces are smaller and more mechanical:

  - Nested procedures (O4) — architectural, but small. Lambda-lifting at MSIR
  construction time is the obvious answer; this walkthrough mostly confirms it.
  - VAR/READONLY parameter ABI (O10) — pure mechanics; reference parameters as
  ptr with nocapture/noalias.
  - SUBARRAY (O13) — the subtle one; forces the GC-liveness refinement (O12).

  After those three, the design covers enough of Modula-3 that we could
  plausibly start building m3-sys/msir — the in-memory IR + textual printer —
  without expecting the spec to lurch on us. The four walkthroughs done already
  cover ~80% of the cross-cutting decisions; the remaining three are localized.

  Want to continue knocking out walkthroughs, or shift to starting the actual 
  m3-sys/msir package so we have a concrete artifact the walkthroughs can
  typecheck against?

✻ Crunched for 3m 1s

❯ Continue knocking out walkthroughs.                                           

⏺ Nested procedures walkthrough.

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  PROCEDURE Outer(threshold: INTEGER): INTEGER =
    VAR count: INTEGER := 0;

    PROCEDURE Inner(n: INTEGER) =
      BEGIN
        IF n > threshold THEN          (* uplevel READ of param *)
          INC(count);                  (* uplevel WRITE of local *)
        END;
      END Inner;

    BEGIN
      Inner(5);
      Inner(15);
      Inner(25);
      RETURN count;
    END Outer;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: a nested procedure with two uplevel accesses — threshold
  read-only (parameter of outer), count read-write (local of outer). Outer calls
   Inner directly three times. M3's language rule that nested procedure values 
  may not escape their enclosing scope (M3 report §4.13) is what makes the
  design clean: no inner-proc value ever needs to be a free-standing first-class
   function pointer.

  2. MSIR

  The decision to make is lambda-lift vs static link. The trade is:

  - Static link: inner proc takes an implicit display argument (pointer to
  outer's frame); uplevel reads become loads through the display. Closer to
  current M3 lowering. Foreign to LLVM (no native concept).
  - Lambda-lift: inner proc becomes a top-level proc with explicit extra
  parameters for the captures. Each capture is passed by value (read-only) or by
   pointer (read-write). LLVM optimizer handles inlining and SSA promotion
  automatically.

  The §4.13 escape restriction makes lambda-lift safe — there is no inner-proc
  value held by a wider scope, so the closure can always be opened up at the
  call site. So lambda-lift at MSIR construction time.

  module Demo

    proc @Outer(threshold: i64) -> i64
    entry:
      ; 'count' is captured by Inner with write access ⇒ must be addressable.
      ; Promote the local to alloca so we can pass &count to Inner.
      %count.slot = alloca i64
      store i64 0, %count.slot

      ; Calls to lambda-lifted Inner pass uplevel state as explicit args.
      ; threshold: read-only capture → by value
      ; count:     read-write capture → by pointer
      call @Outer.Inner(5,  threshold, %count.slot)
      call @Outer.Inner(15, threshold, %count.slot)
      call @Outer.Inner(25, threshold, %count.slot)

      %v = load i64, %count.slot
      ret %v

    ; Lambda-lifted form. Lexical name preserved in mangled symbol.
    ; 'internal' linkage: §4.13 guarantees no caller exists outside Outer.
    proc @Outer.Inner(n: i64, _up_threshold: i64, _up_count: ptr i64) -> void
         linkage internal
    entry:
      %gt = icmp sgt n, _up_threshold
      cond_br %gt, do_inc, exit
    do_inc:
      %v  = load i64, _up_count
      %v2 = iadd %v, 1
      store i64 %v2, _up_count
      br exit
    exit:
      ret

  Three things worth noticing:

  - Capture mode is computed by m3front. It already classifies each uplevel
  reference as read-only vs read-write (the existing up_level analysis). MSIR
  just consumes the classification and emits the parameter list. Read-only
  captures pass by value; read-write captures force the source local to be
  address-taken in the outer.
  - %count becomes an alloca, not an SSA value, in the outer. This is the cost
  of having a write-capture: the outer can no longer keep the local in a
  register across the inner call. After Inner is inlined (which LLVM will do for
   internal single-call-site procs), mem2reg reverses the address-take and the
  alloca disappears.
  - Lexical name preserved as a mangled symbol (@Outer.Inner). This is a
  debugging affordance more than a semantic requirement; it makes profiling and
  stack traces readable. The IR itself doesn't care.

  3. LLVM IR

  define i64 @Outer(i64 %threshold) {
  entry:
    %count = alloca i64
    store i64 0, ptr %count

    call void @Outer.Inner(i64  5, i64 %threshold, ptr nonnull %count)
    call void @Outer.Inner(i64 15, i64 %threshold, ptr nonnull %count)
    call void @Outer.Inner(i64 25, i64 %threshold, ptr nonnull %count)

    %v = load i64, ptr %count
    ret i64 %v
  }

  define internal void @Outer.Inner(i64 %n,
                                     i64 %up_threshold,
                                     ptr nocapture noundef %up_count) {
  entry:
    %gt = icmp sgt i64 %n, %up_threshold
    br i1 %gt, label %do_inc, label %exit
  do_inc:
    %v  = load i64, ptr %up_count
    %v2 = add i64 %v, 1
    store i64 %v2, ptr %up_count
    br label %exit
  exit:
    ret void
  }

  Annotations:

  - internal linkage on @Outer.Inner. §4.13 guarantees no caller outside the
  translation unit. Lets LLVM inline aggressively, dead-code-eliminate if
  unreached, and consider full DCE if all callers are removed.
  - nocapture noundef on %up_count. Inner never stores the pointer, never
  returns it. LLVM can use this for alias analysis around %count.
  - nonnull on the call sites' %count argument: it's an alloca, never null.
  - After -O1: Inner is fully inlined into Outer; mem2reg promotes %count; the
  result is straight-line SSA over an i64 accumulator. Identical to what
  hand-written flat code would produce.

  4. Captured traced refs (briefly)

  The example uses INTEGER captures; the traced-ref case is worth noting in
  passing because it interacts with the GC contract.

  - Read-only capture of a gc_ref T: pass by value. Inner receives ptr 
  addrspace(1). No barrier needed — the value is SSA-resident in Inner just as
  it was in Outer.
  - Read-write capture of a gc_ref T: pass by pointer to outer's stack slot. The
   slot is on the stack (addrspace 0), but its contents are ptr addrspace(1).
  Inner gets ptr addrspace(0) to the slot. Reading and writing the slot is plain
   load/store (no barrier — stack slots aren't subject to the from-space
  invariant), but the slot itself is part of Outer's conservative stack scan:
  the collector finds the ptr addrspace(1) value inside it via the existing
  scan.

  The same shape applies to VAR parameters (O10) — both are "pointer to a
  stack-resident slot whose contents may be traced." That's not a coincidence;
  the next walkthrough will collapse them.

  5. Decisions this walkthrough forces

  1. Nested procedures are lambda-lifted at MSIR construction. No static link,
  no display register. Captures become explicit parameters.
  2. m3front's existing capture analysis is reused. Read-only vs read-write
  classification per uplevel name; same data the C/m3back paths already consume.
  3. Lexical naming is preserved in mangled symbols (Outer.Inner). Debugging
  affordance only; IR semantics don't care.
  4. internal linkage on every nested proc. §4.13 guarantees no external caller;
   gives LLVM maximum freedom to inline and DCE.
  5. Read-write captures force the source local to be address-taken (alloca
  rather than SSA). The cost is recovered by mem2reg after inlining.
  6. Frame-struct grouping is a future optimization, not a current requirement.
  When a nested proc has many captures, bundling them into a struct passed by
  single pointer reduces parameter count and improves alias analysis. Defer
  until measured.
  7. Traced-ref captures fall out of the same model. Read-only by value
  (addrspace 1); read-write by pointer to stack slot (addrspace 0 to addrspace 1
   contents). Conservative stack scan finds traced contents through the slot's
  address.

  6. Updates to the spine doc

  - O4 (lambda-lift vs static link) → resolved by D15.
  - D15 (new): nested procs lambda-lifted at MSIR construction; m3front capture
  analysis drives read-only-by-value vs read-write-by-pointer; internal linkage;
   lexical names preserved.
  - O16 (new): frame-struct grouping for procs with many captures. Defer to
  performance work.

⏺ ---
  Now VAR/READONLY parameter ABI. The nested-procedures walkthrough already
  showed half of this — read-write captures are by-reference parameters in
  disguise. So this should be tight.

  VAR/READONLY walkthrough

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  (* VAR parameter: caller passes an addressable location; callee may write *)
  PROCEDURE Swap(VAR a, b: INTEGER) =
    VAR tmp: INTEGER;
    BEGIN
      tmp := a;
      a := b;
      b := tmp;
    END Swap;

  (* READONLY parameter: avoid copying a large record; callee may not write *)
  TYPE Big = RECORD x, y, z: INTEGER; tag: TEXT END;

  PROCEDURE Sum(READONLY r: Big): INTEGER =
    BEGIN
      RETURN r.x + r.y + r.z;
    END Sum;

  (* VAR of traced-ref type: caller's slot, callee may overwrite *)
  TYPE T = OBJECT n: INTEGER END;

  PROCEDURE Replace(VAR slot: T; new: T) =
    BEGIN
      slot := new;                      (* gc.store into caller's slot *)
    END Replace;

  PROCEDURE Caller() =
    VAR i, j: INTEGER;
        big: Big;
        obj: T;
    BEGIN
      Swap(i, j);                       (* VAR INTEGER *)
      EVAL Sum(big);                    (* READONLY Big — no copy *)
      Replace(obj, NEW(T));             (* VAR T (traced) *)
    END Caller;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: VAR of scalar (must be addressable in caller); READONLY
  of large record (compiler may pass by reference even though semantics are
  by-value); VAR of traced-ref type (caller's slot is in addrspace 0 but
  contents are addrspace 1).

  2. MSIR

  module Demo

    type Big = record { x: i64, y: i64, z: i64, tag: gc_ref Text }
    type T   = object { fields: { n: i64 }, ... descriptor: @T_ti }

    proc @Swap(a: ptr i64 var, b: ptr i64 var) -> void
    entry:
      %va = load i64, a
      %vb = load i64, b
      store i64 %vb, a
      store i64 %va, b
      ret

    proc @Sum(r: ptr Big readonly) -> i64
    entry:
      %xp = field_addr r, Big.x       ; field_addr on a ptr-to-record yields ptr
      %x  = load i64, %xp
      %yp = field_addr r, Big.y
      %y  = load i64, %yp
      %zp = field_addr r, Big.z
      %z  = load i64, %zp
      %s1 = iadd %x, %y
      %s2 = iadd %s1, %z
      ret %s2

    proc @Replace(slot: ptr (gc_ref T) var, new: gc_ref T) -> void
    entry:
      ; The slot is an addrspace(0) pointer (caller's stack), but its contents
      ; are gc_ref T (addrspace 1). Writing to the slot is a gc.store on the
      ; *contents*, not on the slot pointer itself.
      gc.store_through slot, new
      ret

    proc @Caller() -> void
    entry:
      %i.s   = alloca i64                 ; addressable for VAR
      %j.s   = alloca i64
      %big.s = alloca Big                 ; address-taken for READONLY-by-ref
      %obj.s = alloca gc_ref T            ; addressable for VAR;
  conservative-scanned
      store i64 0, %i.s
      store i64 0, %j.s
      store gc_ref T nil, %obj.s

      call @Swap(%i.s, %j.s)
      call @Sum(%big.s) -> _
      %new = new T
      call @Replace(%obj.s, %new)
      ret

  Two distinctions made explicit at MSIR level:

  - ptr T var vs ptr T readonly: the parameter type carries the M3-level mode.
  var permits stores through the pointer; readonly does not. The verifier
  enforces this; the lowering uses it to pick LLVM attributes.
  - gc.store_through slot: the slot is addrspace(0) (stack), but its contents
  are a traced ref. The store needs the write barrier (in case the contents are
  ever scanned — they will be, by the conservative stack scan, but a
  generational write barrier may still apply if the slot has tenured). One op,
  lowering decides whether to inline the barrier.

  3. LLVM IR

  %Big = type { i64, i64, i64, ptr addrspace(1) }
  %T   = type { ptr, i64 }                         ; { typecell, n }

  define void @Swap(ptr nocapture noundef %a, ptr nocapture noundef %b) {
    %va = load i64, ptr %a
    %vb = load i64, ptr %b
    store i64 %vb, ptr %a
    store i64 %va, ptr %b
    ret void
  }

  define i64 @Sum(ptr nocapture noundef readonly %r) {
    %xp = getelementptr %Big, ptr %r, i32 0, i32 0
    %x  = load i64, ptr %xp
    %yp = getelementptr %Big, ptr %r, i32 0, i32 1
    %y  = load i64, ptr %yp
    %zp = getelementptr %Big, ptr %r, i32 0, i32 2
    %z  = load i64, ptr %zp
    %s1 = add i64 %x, %y
    %s2 = add i64 %s1, %z
    ret i64 %s2
  }

  declare void @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr, ptr addrspace(1))

  define void @Replace(ptr nocapture noundef %slot, ptr addrspace(1) %new) {
    ; gc.store_through: write barrier with target=*slot (which is a gc_ref),
    ; then store. Barrier-call form (inline form is the fast path).
    call void @RTHeap__WriteBarrier(ptr %slot, ptr addrspace(1) %new)
    store ptr addrspace(1) %new, ptr %slot
    ret void
  }

  declare ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr)
  @T_ti = external constant i8

  define void @Caller() {
    %i.s   = alloca i64
    %j.s   = alloca i64
    %big.s = alloca %Big
    %obj.s = alloca ptr addrspace(1)
    store i64 0, ptr %i.s
    store i64 0, ptr %j.s
    store ptr addrspace(1) null, ptr %obj.s

    call void @Swap(ptr nonnull %i.s, ptr nonnull %j.s)
    %_ = call i64 @Sum(ptr nonnull %big.s)
    %new = call ptr addrspace(1) @RTAllocator__New(ptr @T_ti)
    call void @Replace(ptr nonnull %obj.s, ptr addrspace(1) %new)
    ret void
  }

  Annotations:

  - nocapture on every VAR/READONLY parameter: Modula-3 reference parameters
  cannot be retained beyond the call (no aliasing escape).
  - readonly attribute on Sum's %r parameter: M3 READONLY forbids writes through
   the pointer; the LLVM attribute lets the optimizer treat the call as not
  modifying the pointed-to memory.
  - nonnull at every call site: alloca pointers are never null.
  - No noalias by default. M3 doesn't forbid two VAR parameters from aliasing
  each other (Swap(x, x) is legal and well-defined). Adding noalias is unsound.
  If m3front can prove non-aliasing at a specific call site (e.g. distinct WITH
  bindings), the call site can be tagged.
  - %obj.s as alloca ptr addrspace(1): an addrspace(0) slot whose contents are
  addrspace(1). Conservative stack scan finds the inner pointer through the
  stack location.

  4. Decisions surfaced

  1. var and readonly are MSIR-level parameter modes, not just lowering hints.
  The verifier enforces them; the lowering uses them to emit LLVM attributes.
  2. READONLY of a large aggregate lowers to ptr readonly. m3front decides
  "large" (it already does); MSIR carries the decision.
  3. VAR of a traced ref: pointer is addrspace(0) (caller's stack location),
  contents are addrspace(1). gc.store_through is the dedicated op; the slot
  itself is not a traced ref.
  4. No noalias by default on VAR parameters. M3 permits aliasing reference
  parameters. Per-call-site annotation when m3front can prove non-aliasing.
  5. nocapture is universal on M3 reference parameters. They cannot escape the
  call.
  6. Capture-by-pointer (D15) and VAR (this) collapse to the same lowering. Both
   are "pointer to caller's stack slot, callee may write, callee may not
  capture." The IR has one mechanism, two source-language entry points.

  Updates to spine doc

  - O10 (VAR/READONLY parameter lowering) → resolved by D16.
  - D16 (new): VAR/READONLY are MSIR-level parameter modes; lower to ptr with
  nocapture (universal) and readonly (when M3 says so); VAR of traced ref uses
  addrspace(0) slot pointer with addrspace(1) contents and gc.store_through; no
  default noalias since M3 permits aliasing VAR parameters.

⏺ Last MVP-blocking walkthrough: SUBARRAY — the subtle one.

  SUBARRAY walkthrough

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  PROCEDURE Sum(a: ARRAY OF INTEGER): INTEGER = ...    (* from earlier
  walkthrough *)
  PROCEDURE Make(n: INTEGER): REF ARRAY OF INTEGER = ...

  PROCEDURE SumRange(a: ARRAY OF INTEGER; lo, count: INTEGER): INTEGER =
    VAR sub := SUBARRAY(a, lo, count);
    BEGIN
      RETURN Sum(sub);
    END SumRange;

  PROCEDURE Demo() =
    VAR r   := Make(100);                  (* REF ARRAY OF INTEGER *)
        s   := SUBARRAY(r^, 10, 50);       (* subarray of heap data *)
    BEGIN
      EVAL Sum(s);
      (* r must remain live through Sum(s) — interior pointer keeps it *)
    END Demo;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: SUBARRAY of a parameter-shaped open array (already a fat
  pointer); SUBARRAY of a heap-derived open array (data pointer interior to a
  GC-managed object); cross-procedure call with a fat pointer whose data was
  originally in addrspace(1).

  M3 SUBARRAY is 1D only — multi-dim slicing isn't a thing in the language.
  Saves us a problem.

  2. MSIR

  proc @SumRange(a: openarray<1> i64, lo: i64, count: i64) -> i64
  entry:
    %sub = subarray a, lo, count          ; bounds-checked; arithmetic +
  fat-pointer construct
    %s   = call @Sum(%sub) -> i64
    ret %s

  proc @Demo() -> void
  entry:
    %r    = call @Make(100) -> gc_ref @RArrInt
    %a.r  = openarray.deref %r            ; openarray<1> i64 with data ptr in
  addrspace(1)
    %s    = subarray %a.r, 10, 50         ; result inherits addrspace(1) data
  ptr
    ; %r dominates this call by lexical scope; verifier checks it.
    call @Sum(%s) -> _
    ret

  The op:

  - subarray oa, lo, count — single MSIR op. At lowering it expands to:
    - subscript_check lo, oa.size (lo within range)
    - subscript_check (lo + count - 1), oa.size (last element within range)
    - gep to compute data + lo
    - struct construction { new_data, count }
  - Result inherits the data-pointer address space of the source. Stack-derived
  sources stay in addrspace(0); heap-derived sources stay in addrspace(1).
  - Verifier rule: when an open array's data pointer is in addrspace(1), the
  originating gc_ref (the source REF, or whatever transitively owns the heap
  object) must dominate every use of the open array. This is a lexical-liveness
  check m3front can compute trivially.

  3. LLVM IR

  SumRange (parameter-shaped source — boring case):

  define i64 @SumRange({ ptr, i64 } %a, i64 %lo, i64 %count) {
  entry:
    %data = extractvalue { ptr, i64 } %a, 0
    %size = extractvalue { ptr, i64 } %a, 1

    ; bounds: 0 <= lo && lo+count <= size
    %lo_ok  = icmp sge i64 %lo, 0
    %end    = add nsw i64 %lo, %count
    %end_ok = icmp ule i64 %end, %size
    %ok     = and i1 %lo_ok, %end_ok
    br i1 %ok, label %good, label %fail
  fail:
    call void @RTException__Raise(ptr @SubscriptOutOfRange)
    unreachable
  good:
    %sub.data = getelementptr i64, ptr %data, i64 %lo
    %sub.0    = insertvalue { ptr, i64 } poison,  ptr %sub.data, 0
    %sub.1    = insertvalue { ptr, i64 } %sub.0,  i64 %count,    1
    %r        = call i64 @Sum({ ptr, i64 } %sub.1)
    ret i64 %r
  }

  Demo (heap-derived source — interesting case):

  define void @Demo() {
  entry:
    %r = call ptr addrspace(1) @Make(i64 100)

    ; openarray.deref %r:
    %r.size.p = getelementptr %RArrInt, ptr addrspace(1) %r, i32 0, i32 1
    %r.size   = load i64, ptr addrspace(1) %r.size.p, !invariant.load !0
    %r.data.p = getelementptr %RArrInt, ptr addrspace(1) %r, i32 0, i32 2, i64 0

    ; subarray r^, 10, 50 — constants fold:
    %ok = icmp ule i64 60, %r.size
    br i1 %ok, label %good, label %fail
  fail:
    call void @RTException__Raise(ptr @SubscriptOutOfRange)
    unreachable
  good:
    ; data ptr stays in addrspace(1)...
    %sub.data.h = getelementptr i64, ptr addrspace(1) %r.data.p, i64 10

    ; ...but the @Sum ABI takes data in addrspace(0). One cast at the boundary:
    %sub.data = addrspacecast ptr addrspace(1) %sub.data.h to ptr

    %sub.0    = insertvalue { ptr, i64 } poison, ptr %sub.data, 0
    %sub.1    = insertvalue { ptr, i64 } %sub.0, i64 50,         1
    %_        = call i64 @Sum({ ptr, i64 } %sub.1)
    ret void
  }

  !0 = !{}

  Two important annotations:

  - addrspacecast from 1 to 0 at the ABI boundary. Open-array parameters use a
  uniform addrspace(0) data pointer in their ABI shape; the cast at the call
  site is the documented loss-of-tracking point. At runtime the cast is a no-op
  — pointers don't actually carry an address space, that's a compile-time
  annotation only — so the conservative scanner sees the byte value on the stack
   and checks heap pages regardless. The cast just tells the LLVM optimizer
  "from here on, treat this as untraced."
  - Pinning is doing the work. %sub.data is an interior pointer into the heap
  object that %r references. Bartlett-style collectors handle this: any pointer
  landing on a heap page pins the containing page. So even if LLVM
  register-allocates %r away after %sub.data is computed, the conservative scan
  finds %sub.data and pins. This is the place where we are leaning on the
  existing collector's interior-pointer support, and it's the same lean the C
  backend already takes — we are not introducing new fragility, just preserving
  the existing equilibrium.

  4. Decisions surfaced

  1. SUBARRAY is a single MSIR op that lowers to bounds-check + GEP +
  fat-pointer construction.
  2. Open-array data-pointer address space is preserved through SUBARRAY but
  lost at cross-procedure call boundaries via addrspacecast. This is the
  uniform-ABI choice — open-array parameters are always addrspace(0) at the ABI.
  3. Heap-derived subarrays rely on conservative interior-pointer pinning
  (Bartlett's mechanism). This is the same dependency the C backend already has;
   MSIR does not introduce or worsen it.
  4. MSIR verifier enforces lexical dominance: when an open array carries an
  addrspace(1) data pointer, the originating gc_ref must dominate every use of
  the open array. This is a static check, not a runtime keepalive.
  5. The proper fix for the addrspacecast is statepoints (O11). With statepoint
  integration, traced refs stay in addrspace(1) across calls; the addrspacecast
  disappears; precise tracking replaces conservative pinning. The MSIR shape
  doesn't change.

  5. Updates to the spine doc

  - O13 (SUBARRAY) → resolved by D17.
  - O12 (GC liveness of derived pointers) → refined: the specific hazard is the
  addrspacecast at the ABI boundary; mitigation is conservative interior-pointer
   pinning today, statepoint adoption later. Link to O11.

⏺ ---
  Where we are now

  Six walkthroughs done: OBJECT, TRY, opens, module init, nested procs,
  VAR/READONLY, SUBARRAY (the last three in this turn). The spine doc tracks 17 
  decisions and 16 open questions, with the MVP-blocking questions all resolved
  or contained:

  - D1–D17 cover object layout, dispatch, GC contract, EH contract, module init,
   nested procs, reference parameters, and SUBARRAY.
  - Open questions that remain are either performance work (O2 NARROW algorithm,
   O11 statepoints, O16 frame-struct grouping), tooling (O7 LLVM version, O8
  bootstrap path), localized features (O5 LOCK, O14 unhandled-init exceptions,
  O15 generics, O9 unsafe constructs), or deferred-to-statepoints (O12
  derived-pointer liveness, contained for MVP).

  The MVP-blocking walkthroughs are done. The remaining language pieces (sets,
  subrange, bitfields, NARROW/TYPECASE/ISTYPE, FOR vs LOOP+EXIT, WITH bindings,
  generics, unsafe) are either:
  - Mechanical applications of decisions already made (sets, bitfields,
  FOR/LOOP, WITH);
  - Deferred-by-design (generics, unsafe);
  - Performance refinements (NARROW algorithms).

  So the next inflection point is starting the actual m3-sys/msir package — the
  typed in-memory IR + textual printer that the walkthroughs would round-trip
  through. That would force concretization of decisions that are still slightly
  hand-wavy (exact opcode names, exact type-syntax, value-numbering scheme), and
   give us a falsification target.

  Want to start that, or do another walkthrough first (NARROW/TYPECASE/ISTYPE
  would be the highest-leverage one — it's the place where MSIR's intrinsic
  surface really pays off)?

✻ Baked for 5m 33s

❯ Let's don that last walkthrough.                                              

⏺ NARROW / ISTYPE / TYPECASE walkthrough.

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  TYPE
    Animal = OBJECT name: TEXT END;
    Dog    = Animal OBJECT breed: TEXT END;
    Cat    = Animal OBJECT END;
    Puppy  = Dog OBJECT END;

  PROCEDURE IsDog(a: Animal): BOOLEAN =
    BEGIN
      RETURN ISTYPE(a, Dog);
    END IsDog;

  PROCEDURE BreedOf(a: Animal): TEXT =
    BEGIN
      RETURN NARROW(a, Dog).breed;
    END BreedOf;

  PROCEDURE Describe(a: Animal): TEXT =
    BEGIN
      TYPECASE a OF
      | Puppy       => RETURN "puppy"
      | Dog (d)     => RETURN d.breed         (* d bound to Dog-typed view *)
      | Cat         => RETURN "cat"
      ELSE             RETURN "unknown"
      END;
    END Describe;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: predicate (ISTYPE), assertive downcast (NARROW),
  structured dispatch with value binding and clause ordering (TYPECASE). Note
  clause order: Puppy precedes Dog because Puppy <: Dog and TYPECASE matches the
   first clause that fits.

  2. MSIR

  module Demo

    type Animal = object { fields: { name: gc_slot Text } ... descriptor:
  @Animal_ti }
    type Dog    = Animal object { fields: { breed: gc_slot Text } ...
  descriptor: @Dog_ti }
    type Cat    = Animal object { fields: { } ... descriptor: @Cat_ti }
    type Puppy  = Dog object    { fields: { } ... descriptor: @Puppy_ti }

    proc @IsDog(a: gc_ref Animal) -> i1
    entry:
      %r = istype a, @Dog_ti
      ret %r

    proc @BreedOf(a: gc_ref Animal) -> gc_ref Text
    entry:
      %d = narrow a, @Dog_ti                  ; raises runtime fault on miss
      %p = field_addr %d, Dog.breed
      %t = gc.load %p
      ret %t

    proc @Describe(a: gc_ref Animal) -> gc_ref Text
    entry:
      typecase a {
        Puppy        => block puppy:
                          ret @str.puppy
        Dog (d)      => block dog(d: gc_ref Dog):
                          %p = field_addr d, Dog.breed
                          %t = gc.load %p
                          ret %t
        Cat          => block cat:
                          ret @str.cat
        ELSE         => block else_b:
                          ret @str.unknown
      }

  Three things worth noticing:

  - All three are first-class MSIR intrinsics, not runtime calls. This is the
  whole point of having an intrinsic surface: when the static type pins the
  answer, MSIR-level peepholes fold them; when it doesn't, the lowering picks an
   algorithm (chain walk for MVP, Schubert / Cohen later) without touching MSIR.
  - narrow is the runtime-faulting form of istype. It has the same algorithmic
  shape but raises on miss instead of returning false. The fault is a Modula-3
  runtime error (not a declared exception), so narrow does not require the
  procedure to declare raises {…}.
  - typecase is structured with parameterized blocks, exactly like the exception
   handlers in the EH walkthrough. Value-binding clauses (Dog(d)) become block
  parameters; lowering retypes the parameter (same address, narrower view). The
  ELSE clause is mandatory at MSIR level — m3front synthesizes a "raise
  TypecaseMismatch" ELSE if the source omits one.

  MSIR-level peepholes (the static-type wins)

  Three peephole rules that fire before lowering:

  1. istype %x, T where %x's MSIR static type is a known subtype of T → i1 1.
  2. narrow %x, T where %x's static type is a subtype of T → trivial bitcast
  (same value, narrower MSIR type).
  3. typecase clause whose istype is constant-true → unconditional branch into
  the clause; remaining clauses become dead.

  These are pure MSIR rewrites, computable from m3front's existing static-type
  information. They cost nothing at lowering time and they're the reason
  MSIR-via-LLVM produces tighter code than M3CG-via-clang for any code path that
   already knows what type it has.

  3. LLVM IR

  For the dynamic case (no peephole fired). I'll factor out the chain-walk into
  a small helper that LLVM can inline once a real algorithm is chosen.

  declare void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32) noreturn
  %RTType = type { ptr, i32, ptr, i32, [0 x ptr] }    ; { super, ... }

  ; --- chain-walk helper. Replaceable by Schubert/Cohen later. ----------------
  define internal i1 @__istype_walk(ptr nocapture %ti, ptr nocapture %target) {
  entry:
    br label %loop
  loop:
    %cur = phi ptr [ %ti, %entry ], [ %super, %step ]
    %match = icmp eq ptr %cur, %target
    br i1 %match, label %ret_true, label %step
  step:
    %sp = getelementptr %RTType, ptr %cur, i32 0, i32 0
    %super = load ptr, ptr %sp, !invariant.load !0
    %end = icmp eq ptr %super, null
    br i1 %end, label %ret_false, label %loop
  ret_true:  ret i1 1
  ret_false: ret i1 0
  }

  ; --- IsDog: ISTYPE 
  -----------------------------------------------------------
  define i1 @IsDog(ptr addrspace(1) %a) {
  entry:
    %is_null = icmp eq ptr addrspace(1) %a, null
    br i1 %is_null, label %ret_false, label %check
  check:
    %hdr = getelementptr %Animal, ptr addrspace(1) %a, i32 0, i32 0
    %ti  = load ptr, ptr addrspace(1) %hdr, !invariant.load !0
    %r   = call i1 @__istype_walk(ptr %ti, ptr @Dog_ti)
    ret i1 %r
  ret_false:
    ret i1 0
  }

  ; --- BreedOf: NARROW + field load -------------------------------------------
  define ptr addrspace(1) @BreedOf(ptr addrspace(1) %a) {
  entry:
    ; NIL passes NARROW for object types (all of them include NIL); the null
    ; check is for the chain walk, which can't run on null. Subsequent .breed
    ; deref on NIL would raise its own RT fault.
    %is_null = icmp eq ptr addrspace(1) %a, null
    br i1 %is_null, label %fail_null_deref, label %check
  check:
    %hdr = getelementptr %Animal, ptr addrspace(1) %a, i32 0, i32 0
    %ti  = load ptr, ptr addrspace(1) %hdr, !invariant.load !0
    %ok  = call i1 @__istype_walk(ptr %ti, ptr @Dog_ti)
    br i1 %ok, label %ok_b, label %fail_narrow
  ok_b:
    ; narrow succeeded: same pointer, view as %Dog
    %bp = getelementptr %Dog, ptr addrspace(1) %a, i32 0, i32 2     ; field 2 = 
  breed
    %t  = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr addrspace(1) %bp                ; (gc.load 
  fast path)
    ret ptr addrspace(1) %t
  fail_narrow:
    call void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32 NARROW_FAULT) noreturn
    unreachable
  fail_null_deref:
    call void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32 NIL_DEREF) noreturn
    unreachable
  }

  ; --- Describe: TYPECASE -----------------------------------------------------
  define ptr addrspace(1) @Describe(ptr addrspace(1) %a) {
  entry:
    %is_null = icmp eq ptr addrspace(1) %a, null
    br i1 %is_null, label %else_b, label %load
  load:
    %hdr = getelementptr %Animal, ptr addrspace(1) %a, i32 0, i32 0
    %ti  = load ptr, ptr addrspace(1) %hdr, !invariant.load !0
    %is_puppy = call i1 @__istype_walk(ptr %ti, ptr @Puppy_ti)
    br i1 %is_puppy, label %puppy, label %try_dog
  try_dog:
    %is_dog = call i1 @__istype_walk(ptr %ti, ptr @Dog_ti)
    br i1 %is_dog, label %dog, label %try_cat
  try_cat:
    %is_cat = call i1 @__istype_walk(ptr %ti, ptr @Cat_ti)
    br i1 %is_cat, label %cat, label %else_b
  puppy:
    ret ptr addrspace(1) @str.puppy
  dog:
    ; d := narrowed view; same address. Field offset for Dog.breed = 2
    %bp = getelementptr %Dog, ptr addrspace(1) %a, i32 0, i32 2
    %t  = load ptr addrspace(1), ptr addrspace(1) %bp
    ret ptr addrspace(1) %t
  cat:
    ret ptr addrspace(1) @str.cat
  else_b:
    ret ptr addrspace(1) @str.unknown
  }

  !0 = !{}

  Annotations:

  - !invariant.load on every typecell pointer load and every super-chain link
  load. CM3 type descriptors are immutable; LLVM may CSE these aggressively
  across the three walks in Describe. Three calls to __istype_walk share one
  typecell load.
  - __istype_walk is internal, single-source-of-truth, three callers in
  Describe. After choosing Schubert numbering (O2), this function gets rewritten
   to two integer comparisons; every caller benefits with no IR-level change.
  - TYPECASE compiled as a chain of istype calls, in source order. With Schubert
   numbering and constant target typecells, LLVM can sometimes turn the chain
  into a single switch on %ti.schubert_low. That's a future LLVM-level pass, not
   a current concern.
  - Value-binding in the dog block is just a GEP — no type-level work needed
  because the address didn't change. d is the same pointer as a, with a
  different MSIR/LLVM type.
  - @__istype_walk does not read traced memory. RTType chains live in immutable
  globals. The function can be marked readnone (or readonly memory(argmem: 
  read)) for further optimization. I left it off above for readability; the real
   lowering should add it.

  4. The static-type win, illustrated

  When MSIR's peephole knows the answer, the lowering changes dramatically.
  Consider:

  PROCEDURE Bark(d: Dog): TEXT = BEGIN RETURN d.breed END Bark;

  PROCEDURE Test() =
    VAR d := NEW(Dog, breed := "lab");
    BEGIN
      IF ISTYPE(d, Dog) THEN EVAL Bark(d) END;     (* ISTYPE always TRUE *)
    END Test;

  After MSIR peephole D18.1 (%d's static type is Dog, target is Dog):

  proc @Test() -> void
  entry:
    %d = new Dog
    field_store %d, Dog.breed, @str.lab
    ; ISTYPE(d, Dog) was folded to i1 1; the IF disappears entirely.
    call @Bark(%d) -> _
    ret

  The runtime chain-walk never appears. The C-backend lowering, by contrast,
  emits the chain walk because clang has no way to know the typecell is
  invariant or that the static type implies the answer.

  This is the kind of payoff D18 buys: the MSIR-level intrinsic surface is the
  place where Modula-3 semantics meet LLVM's reasoning. Folding here is much
  cheaper than trying to recover the same conclusion from M3CG-shaped output.

  5. Decisions surfaced

  1. NARROW / ISTYPE / TYPECASE are first-class MSIR intrinsics, not runtime
  calls. m3front emits them; lowering chooses the algorithm.
  2. The chain-walk algorithm is encapsulated in a single replaceable helper
  (__istype_walk). Schubert numbering / Cohen / etc. are wholesale replacements
  without IR change.
  3. NARROW failure is a Modula-3 runtime error, terminating by default; not
  part of raises {…}. Lowers to a noreturn call to RTHooks__ReportFault.
  4. TYPECASE clause order is preserved at MSIR level. First-match-wins is the
  IR-level rule; m3front determines order; lowering emits in order.
  5. TYPECASE's ELSE clause is mandatory at MSIR level — m3front synthesizes a
  runtime-fault ELSE when the source omits one.
  6. Value-binding clauses (Dog(d)) are parameterized blocks, identical to
  exception handlers. The binding is a retype, not a memory operation.
  7. MSIR-level peepholes fold istype/narrow/typecase when the static type pins
  the answer. These are pre-lowering rewrites.
  8. Typecell pointer loads and super-chain link loads carry !invariant.load.
  LLVM may CSE freely across calls and loops.

  6. Updates to spine doc

  - D18 (new): NARROW/ISTYPE/TYPECASE first-class MSIR intrinsics; algorithm in
  replaceable helper; MSIR-level peephole folds when static types pin the
  answer; clause order preserved; ELSE mandatory.
  - O2 (algorithm choice) stays open — it's a performance tuning concern,
  properly deferred. The walkthrough gives it a clearer constraint: must be a
  wholesale replacement of the chain-walk helper without touching MSIR.

  Where we are now

  Seven walkthroughs done: OBJECT + METHOD, TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY, open arrays,
  module init, nested procedures, VAR/READONLY, SUBARRAY,
  NARROW/TYPECASE/ISTYPE.

  Spine doc: 18 decisions, 16 open questions, with all MVP-blocking pieces
  pinned. Open questions are now exclusively performance work (O2, O11, O16),
  tooling (O7, O8), localized features (O5, O14, O15, O9), or
  contained-by-design (O12).

  The walkthroughs have covered every load-bearing axis of the IR. Remaining
  language pieces (sets, subrange, bitfields, FOR/LOOP/EXIT, WITH bindings) are
  mechanical applications of decisions already made — they don't surface new
  architectural questions. Generics (O15) and unsafe constructs (O9) are
  deferred, but they're language-feature additions that compose with the
  existing IR rather than requiring new design.

  The next inflection point is starting m3-sys/msir — the typed in-memory IR +
  textual printer that the walkthroughs round-trip through. That forces the
  still-hand-wavy bits to become concrete: exact opcode names, exact type-syntax
   grammar, value-numbering scheme, verifier rules, error reporting. It also
  gives us a falsification target — when we sketch MSIR text in a future
  walkthrough, we'll know whether the package can typecheck it.

  Want to start the package, or do one more walkthrough first (sets and subrange
   would be a small one that wraps up the type-system corner)?

❯ One more walkthrough first.                                                   

⏺ Sets and subrange — wraps up the type-system corner.

  1. Source

  MODULE Demo;

  TYPE
    Day     = {Mon, Tue, Wed, Thu, Fri, Sat, Sun};
    DaySet  = SET OF Day;
    Workday = [Day.Mon..Day.Fri];           (* subrange of enum *)
    Pct     = [0..100];                     (* subrange of integer *)

  CONST
    Weekend  = DaySet{Day.Sat, Day.Sun};
    Weekdays = DaySet{Day.Mon..Day.Fri};

  PROCEDURE IsWeekend(d: Day): BOOLEAN =
    BEGIN RETURN d IN Weekend; END IsWeekend;

  PROCEDURE Combine(a, b: DaySet): DaySet =
    BEGIN RETURN a + b; END Combine;        (* union *)

  PROCEDURE Common(a, b: DaySet): DaySet =
    BEGIN RETURN a * b; END Common;         (* intersection *)

  PROCEDURE Cardinality(s: DaySet): INTEGER =
    VAR n: INTEGER := 0;
    BEGIN
      FOR d := FIRST(Day) TO LAST(Day) DO
        IF d IN s THEN INC(n) END;
      END;
      RETURN n;
    END Cardinality;

  PROCEDURE Clamp(v: INTEGER): Pct =
    VAR p: Pct;
    BEGIN
      p := v;                               (* range check on assignment *)
      RETURN p;
    END Clamp;

  PROCEDURE NextWorkday(d: Workday): Day =
    BEGIN
      IF d = Day.Fri THEN RETURN Day.Mon
      ELSE RETURN VAL(ORD(d) + 1, Day);     (* VAL needs range check *)
      END;
    END NextWorkday;

  BEGIN END Demo.

  What this exercises: enum (7 elements); small set type (SET OF Day = 7 bits,
  fits in i8); set construction with elements and ranges; IN, union,
  intersection; the canonical bitset-iteration loop; subrange of integer with
  assignment range check; subrange of enum with ORD/VAL conversions and
  range-checked narrowing.

  2. MSIR

  module Demo

    type Day     = enum<UID=42, n=7>                 ; values 0..6, repr i8
    type DaySet  = set<Day, lo=0, hi=6>              ; repr i8 (7 bits)
    type Workday = subrange<Day, lo=0, hi=4>         ; repr i8
    type Pct     = subrange<i64, lo=0, hi=100>       ; repr i64

    ; ---- constant sets ------------------------------------------------------
    const @Weekend  : DaySet = const_set DaySet, [5, 6]          ; bits 5,6 ⇒ i8
   0x60
    const @Weekdays : DaySet = const_set DaySet, [0..4]          ; bits 0..4 ⇒
  i8 0x1f

    ; ---- procedures ---------------------------------------------------------

    proc @IsWeekend(d: Day) -> i1
    entry:
      %s = load DaySet, @Weekend                     ; sets aren't traced; plain
   load
      %r = set_member %s, d
      ret %r

    proc @Combine(a: DaySet, b: DaySet) -> DaySet
    entry:
      %r = set_union a, b
      ret %r

    proc @Common(a: DaySet, b: DaySet) -> DaySet
    entry:
      %r = set_intersect a, b
      ret %r

    proc @Cardinality(s: DaySet) -> i64
    entry:
      br loop(0, 0)                                  ; (ord_index, count)
    loop(%i: i64, %n: i64):
      %le = icmp sle %i, 6                           ; LAST(Day) = 6
      cond_br %le, body, exit
    body:
      %d  = convert %i to Day                        ; representation no-op
      %m  = set_member s, %d
      %n2 = select %m, iadd(%n, 1), %n
      %i2 = iadd %i, 1
      br loop(%i2, %n2)
    exit:
      ret %n

    proc @Clamp(v: i64) -> Pct
    entry:
      range_check v, 0, 100                          ; raises range fault if out
      %p = convert v to Pct                          ; representation no-op
      ret %p

    proc @NextWorkday(d: Workday) -> Day
    entry:
      %d.ord  = convert d to i64                     ; ORD(d)
      %is_fri = icmp eq %d.ord, 4
      cond_br %is_fri, ret_mon, ret_next
    ret_mon:
      ret Day.Mon
    ret_next:
      %o2 = iadd %d.ord, 1
      range_check %o2, 0, 6                          ; VAL(_, Day) — checks
  parent range
      %r  = convert %o2 to Day
      ret %r

  Three things worth noticing:

  - Sets are scalar at MSIR level when they fit in a word. DaySet is one of
  set<Day, 0, 6>; the lowering picks i8 as the representation. Larger sets
  become aggregate types (array of i64 words), but the MSIR ops are the same —
  only the lowering changes.
  - convert is unchecked; range checks are emitted as explicit range_check ops
  before a narrowing convert. This matches the VAR/READONLY pattern (D16):
  m3front decides what's needed and emits it; MSIR doesn't second-guess. The
  benefit is that LLVM's range analysis can eliminate the check independently of
   the convert.
  - Subrange and enum are MSIR type kinds distinct from their representation.
  Workday and Day both lower to i8, but they're different MSIR types; m3front
  uses the distinction to emit fewer or tighter range checks (e.g. Workday knows
   its values are [0..4], so most uses don't need re-checking). The static-type
  information is what makes the optimizer's range analysis tractable.

  3. LLVM IR

  @Weekend  = constant i8 96      ; 0b01100000 — bits 5 (Sat), 6 (Sun)
  @Weekdays = constant i8 31      ; 0b00011111 — bits 0..4

  declare void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32) noreturn

  ; --- IsWeekend: small-set IN test ---------------------------------------
  define i1 @IsWeekend(i8 %d) {
    %s  = load i8, ptr @Weekend
    ; set_member: bit test = (s >> d) & 1
    %sh = lshr i8 %s, %d
    %m  = and i8 %sh, 1
    %r  = icmp ne i8 %m, 0
    ret i1 %r
  }

  ; --- set_union / set_intersect: bitwise on small sets -------------------
  define i8 @Combine(i8 %a, i8 %b) {
    %r = or i8 %a, %b
    ret i8 %r
  }

  define i8 @Common(i8 %a, i8 %b) {
    %r = and i8 %a, %b
    ret i8 %r
  }

  ; --- Cardinality: explicit loop, LLVM may recognize as ctpop ------------
  define i64 @Cardinality(i8 %s) {
  entry:
    br label %loop
  loop:
    %i = phi i64 [ 0, %entry ], [ %i.next, %body ]
    %n = phi i64 [ 0, %entry ], [ %n.next, %body ]
    %cont = icmp sle i64 %i, 6
    br i1 %cont, label %body, label %exit
  body:
    %d.i8 = trunc i64 %i to i8                    ; convert i64 to Day (no-op at
   runtime)
    %sh = lshr i8 %s, %d.i8
    %m  = and i8 %sh, 1
    %is_in = icmp ne i8 %m, 0
    %inc  = zext i1 %is_in to i64
    %n.next = add i64 %n, %inc
    %i.next = add i64 %i, 1
    br label %loop
  exit:
    ret i64 %n
  }

  ; --- Clamp: range_check 0..100, then narrow to Pct ----------------------
  define i64 @Clamp(i64 %v) {
  entry:
    ; unsigned-compare trick: catches negative AND > 100 in one icmp.
    %too_big = icmp ugt i64 %v, 100
    br i1 %too_big, label %fail, label %ok
  ok:
    ret i64 %v                                    ; Pct repr is i64; convert 
  no-op
  fail:
    call void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32 RANGE_FAULT)
    unreachable
  }

  ; --- NextWorkday: ORD + add + range_check + VAL -------------------------
  define i8 @NextWorkday(i8 %d) {
  entry:
    %is_fri = icmp eq i8 %d, 4
    br i1 %is_fri, label %ret_mon, label %ret_next
  ret_mon:
    ret i8 0
  ret_next:
    %o2 = add i8 %d, 1
    ; range_check 0..6 on %o2. Given input is Workday [0..4] and not 4 here ⇒ %d
   in [0..3] ⇒ %o2 in [1..4].
    ; LLVM ScalarEvolution / IPSCCP can prove this and eliminate the check.
    %too_big = icmp ugt i8 %o2, 6
    br i1 %too_big, label %fail, label %ok
  ok:
    ret i8 %o2
  fail:
    call void @RTHooks__ReportFault(i32 RANGE_FAULT)
    unreachable
  }

  Annotations:

  - @Weekend and @Weekdays are bare constant i8 globals — set construction at
  compile time folds to a single byte, no init code. This is what const_set in
  MSIR enables: m3front already computes the bit pattern; MSIR carries it
  forward as a constant initializer.
  - Cardinality's loop is the textbook bitset count. LLVM's loop-idiom
  recognizer can fold for i in 0..N: count += (s >> i) & 1 into llvm.ctpop.
  Recognition isn't guaranteed at every optimization level but works in practice
   for small fixed bounds. If it doesn't fire, the loop runs as written — still
  7 iterations, fully unrollable.
  - The unsigned-compare trick (icmp ugt) handles both bounds in one instruction
   when lo == 0. For a general range_check v, lo, hi, the lowering subtracts lo
  first: icmp ugt (sub v, lo), (sub hi, lo). Standard pattern.
  - range_check in NextWorkday is provably eliminable but stays in the IR for
  the optimizer to remove. m3front does not need to do range analysis itself —
  emit conservatively, let LLVM do its job. This is one of the wins of carrying
  subrange in the IR: LLVM's range analysis sees Workday is [0..4], the Fri
  branch removes 4, increment maps to [1..4], range check [0..6] always passes,
  check disappears.
  - No barrier on load @Weekend. Sets aren't traced; the global is in
  addrspace(0), plain load.

  4. Larger sets

  Sets larger than the target word are aggregate types in MSIR — set<T, 0, 1023>
   lowers to an LLVM [16 x i64] (1024 bits). The MSIR ops don't change shape;
  the lowering does:

  - set_union/set_intersect → loop over words, or unrolled or/and for
  known-small.
  - set_member s, d → let (w, b) = (d >> 6, d & 63); (s[w] >> b) & 1.
  - const_set → constant array initializer.

  For sets that exceed an inline threshold (say, 4 words), the lowering can call
   into m3core's existing set-helper procedures rather than inline. The MSIR
  text doesn't reveal which choice the lowering made; that's the lowering's
  policy decision.

  5. Decisions surfaced

  1. Sets are MSIR-typed: set<T, lo, hi>. Small sets lower to integer scalars;
  large sets to aggregate [N x i64].
  2. Set ops are intrinsics: set_union, set_intersect, set_difference,
  set_member, set_singleton, set_construct, set_card. Lowerings inline for
  small, runtime call for large.
  3. const_set carries the bit pattern at MSIR level. m3front computes it;
  lowering emits a constant.
  4. Subrange is an MSIR type kind distinct from its representation. subrange<T,
   lo, hi> carries bounds; representation is chosen by m3front (smallest fitting
   integer of the parent kind).
  5. range_check is an explicit op separate from convert. m3front emits a
  range_check before a narrowing convert when narrowing semantics demand it.
  Same pattern as VAR/READONLY (D16) and subscript_check (D13).
  6. convert is unchecked — pure representation change. ORD/VAL are convert ops;
   VAL on enum/subrange may have a preceding range_check.
  7. Set cardinality emits an explicit loop, not an intrinsic. LLVM's loop-idiom
   recognizer maps it to llvm.ctpop when the shape allows. Adding a dedicated
  set_card MSIR op is a future optimization if recognition proves unreliable.

  6. Updates to spine doc

  - D19 (new): sets and subrange are typed in MSIR; set ops are intrinsics with
  size-conditional lowering; range checks are explicit and separate from
  converts; representations chosen by m3front.

  Where we are now

  Eight walkthroughs done: OBJECT + METHOD, TRY/EXCEPT/FINALLY, open arrays,
  module init, nested procedures, VAR/READONLY, SUBARRAY,
  NARROW/TYPECASE/ISTYPE, sets + subrange.

  Spine doc: 19 decisions, 16 open questions. Every type-system kind is now
  pinned. Every load-bearing IR shape is decided. Every M3-distinctive intrinsic
   has a defined op and lowering policy.

  What remains as walkthrough material is genuinely small:
  - Bitfields / packed records — mechanical extension of the field/load/store
  ops with bit-aligned variants. No new architectural questions.
  - FOR / LOOP / EXIT — flat CFG mechanics; EXIT from inside a TRY is unwind_to
  (already pinned in A8/D8).
  - WITH bindings — alias annotations on locals; reuses VAR/READONLY mechanics
  (D16).
  - TEXT and string literals — just a global ptr in addrspace(1) with the
  literal-text discriminator; mechanical.
  - Generics (O15) and unsafe constructs (O9) — deferred by design.

  These are mechanical applications, not design exercises. Doing more
  walkthroughs gives diminishing returns.

  The next inflection point — and the right next step — is starting the actual 
  m3-sys/msir package: typed in-memory IR, builders, verifier, textual
  printer/parser. That forces the still-implicit decisions (exact opcode names,
  type-syntax grammar, value-numbering scheme, error reporting) to become
  concrete. It also gives us a falsification target — when we sketch MSIR text
  in a future walkthrough, we'll know whether the package can typecheck it.

  Want to start that next?
